## management-governance — module synthesis

_Source: `99_management-governance-synthesis.md`_

# Management-Governance Module — AMZN (Synthesis)

**Date:** 2026-07-04
**Ticker:** AMZN (Amazon.com, Inc., NasdaqGS)
**Reporting standard:** US GAAP. **Currency:** USD. **Fiscal year end:** December 31.

---

## Abstract

Amazon is run by aligned, competent stewards who have delivered on their promises, though two structural limitations prevent an "exemplary" verdict. Over FY2022–FY2025, operating income grew 6.5x (from $12.2B to $80.0B), AWS re-accelerated from 13% to 28% growth, and every multi-year commitment in the three shareholder letters under review was kept or exceeded. Capital allocation has been value-creating: ROIC ran at 18–20% in FY2024–FY2025 and incremental ROIC peaked at 35% in FY2023, though the $131.8B FY2025 capex surge — guided to approximately $200B in FY2026 — compresses near-term free cash flow and is the single largest unresolved execution risk. Incentives are structurally unusual: no annual bonus, no performance conditions on RSU vesting, and a $212M CEO grant that back-loads over ten years; this removes short-term earnings-management pressure but ties vesting purely to time served, not demonstrated returns. Founder Jeff Bezos holds ~8.8% as pure founder equity and represents the deepest form of insider alignment, while all other officers hold only grant-origin shares with no open-market purchases. The most important governance risk is the persistent ~22% institutional opposition to the compensation structure (say-on-pay peaked at 44% against in 2022, plateaued at 22% in 2024–2025, then improved to ~6% in 2026) and a $6.1B buyback authorization that has sat dormant for three years while RSU vesting dilutes shares at ~1.7% per year. The verdict is aligned and competent stewardship, with the $200B AI capex bet the only unresolved binary risk to that verdict.

---

## 1. Stewardship Verdict

- **Verdict:** Aligned & competent

- **Hard disqualifier flagged (business-model/01)?** **No.** All eight disqualifiers from `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` are clear. Zero verdict lock. Explicit disqualifier scan verdict: "No disqualifier triggered. Proceed to deeper analysis."

- Management quality /100: **88**
- Capital allocation /100: **73**
- Incentive alignment /100: **50** *(capped at 50 — no proxy / compensation disclosure in pool; pre-cap score 60)*
- Shareholder friendliness /100: **60** *(capped at 60 — no complete beneficial ownership table in pool; agent 05 structural-governance score 85, ownership agent score 83; cap applied per MODULE_RULES)*
- Disclosure candor /100: **87**
- Governance risk /100 *(higher = worse, inverted)*: **22** *(low-risk profile; one High RF-CAP-002 and one High RF-SHR-001 triggered; no Critical red flags)*
- Data quality /100: **72** *(from 00_governance-data-triage: three annual 10-Ks, Q1 2026 10-Q, five transcripts, Capital IQ governance exports all present; proxy absent, partial ownership data)*
- Overall usefulness /100: **70** *(capped at 70 — no proxy / compensation disclosure per MODULE_RULES)*

- Insider ownership (one line): Bezos ~8.8% (pure founder equity, declining slowly via 10b5-1 plans); all other named insiders <0.5% (RSU grants only; no open-market purchases) [FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74; Capital IQ Public Company Profile, Jul 1, 2026]

- Biggest governance signal (one line): Operating income grew 6.5x from $12.2B (FY2022) to $80.0B (FY2025) — every multi-year promise checked against filings was kept or exceeded, and the FCF compression was proactively pre-disclosed [FY2022–FY2025 10-K; FY2024 Annual Report Shareholder Letter]

- **Governance Score /100** — formula: `0.20×CapAlloc + 0.18×Incentive + 0.18×ShFriendliness + 0.16×Candor + 0.16×MgmtQuality + 0.12×(100 − GovRisk)`:
  - Inputs: CapAlloc=73, Incentive=50 (capped), ShFriendliness=60 (capped), Candor=87, MgmtQuality=88, GovRisk=22
  - 0.20×73 = 14.60
  - 0.18×50 = 9.00
  - 0.18×60 = 10.80
  - 0.16×87 = 13.92
  - 0.16×88 = 14.08
  - 0.12×(100−22) = 0.12×78 = 9.36
  - **Governance Score = 71.76 → 72**

- **Confidence-Adjusted Governance Score /100** = 72 × 75/100 = **54**

- **Governance Rating:** **Good** (72 falls in the 70–84 band)

- **Confidence Score /100:** **75** *(filings and transcripts for management quality and candor: Confidence 5; capital allocation has full 5-year data: Confidence 5; compensation is partially web-sourced with proxy absent: Confidence 2–3; ownership is partially web-sourced: Confidence 2–3; board data partially web-sourced: Confidence 3; blended confidence is approximately 75, reflecting strong filing evidence in the majority of areas and partial-confidence in the proxy-dependent areas)*

- **Red-Flag Count: 2. Critical: 0.**
  - RF-CAP-002 (High): Buybacks not reducing share count — $6.1B of $10B authorization dormant; net dilution +5.0% over five years
  - RF-SHR-001 (High): High say-on-pay votes-against — 22% at 2024 and 2025 meetings (threshold: >20%); improved to ~6% in 2026

---

## 1A. Module Disconfirmation *(CLAUDE.md §8)*

- **Strongest bear point:** The $200B FY2026 capex plan (guided explicitly in the FY2025 shareholder letter) has no formal ROIC floor commitment, no maintenance/growth capex split, and no quantified AI-specific revenue within AWS. If AI infrastructure demand materializes more slowly than the $364B backlog implies — or if model economics shift to favor a smaller number of hyperscalers other than Amazon — incremental ROIC on this investment could fall below 12% (it was already 15.9% in FY2025, down from 24.8% in FY2024), and management's capital-allocation thesis fails. The buyback authorization's continued dormancy during a period of ~1.7%/year share dilution amplifies this: shareholders bear the full dilution cost with no buyback protection if the capex bet underdelivers. [FY2025 Shareholder Letter, Apr 9, 2026; 02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md findings 02-011, 02-006]

- **Strongest bull point:** Six of six multi-year promises were kept or exceeded (FY2023–FY2025). The prior AWS capex build cycle (2016–2019) resulted in exactly the FCF-compression-then-recovery pattern management references. AWS backlog of $364B at Q1 2026 close ($128.7B was FY2025 revenue) implies contracted multi-year demand; and operating income grew 6.5x in three years on a management team that had zero CEO/CFO turnover. These are actions and numbers, not narrative. [01_management-and-track-record.md §4; Q1 2026 10-Q; FY2025 10-K]

- **Single killer risk specific to stewardship/governance:** The compensation structure contains no performance hurdle on RSU vesting. A management team that delivered zero improvement in returns-on-capital — or presided over the AWS capex bet failing to earn above cost of capital — would still vest their full RSU grants as long as they remained employed. The check on this is entirely market-based (stock price), not structural (no ROIC minimum, no TSR threshold, no operating income condition). The risk is not that Jassy is currently misaligned — he demonstrably is not — but that the structure provides no automatic governor if performance deteriorates. [03_incentives-and-compensation.md finding 03-007]

- **Disconfirming evidence already visible:** (1) The FY2022 incremental ROIC was −37.3% (COVID over-build) — evidence that management can over-invest; the current AI capex plan is at least 2x larger in absolute dollar terms than that over-build. (2) Server useful-life changed direction twice in two consecutive years on a $300B+ asset base; the optics were never proactively addressed in transcripts. (3) Say-on-pay opposition averaged 22% for two consecutive years despite strong operating performance — a sustained institutional signal of concern. [02-011, 06-007, 05-016 findings]

## 3. Reconciliation

**No material disagreements between specialists**, with one area requiring explicit reconciliation:

**Shareholder friendliness (agents 04 and 05 vs. synthesis):** Agent 05 (board and shareholder rights) scored this dimension 85/100 based on structural governance features (no dual-class, no staggered board, no poison pill, majority voting). Agent 04 (ownership and insider behavior) scored the ownership alignment 83/100 with a partial-data cap note. The MODULE_RULES partial-data cap for shareholder friendliness is max 60 (no complete beneficial ownership table in pool). The synthesis applies the more conservative cap and uses 60/100. This is not a factual disagreement but a data-completeness constraint: the structural governance features are genuinely strong (agent 05 is right about the facts), but without the formal proxy confirming exact ownership percentages, committee compositions, and voting-results details, the MODULE_RULES cap of 60 must be applied. If the 2026 DEF 14A were added to the pool and confirmed these findings, this score would likely recover to 75–80.

**Capital allocation (agent 02) vs. management quality (agent 01):** Agent 02 scores capital allocation at 73/100 with RF-CAP-002 (buyback dormancy / net dilution) triggered. Agent 01 scores management quality at 88/100, noting the buyback dormancy but treating it as a defensible opportunity-cost trade-off given the 18–20% ROIC reinvestment environment. These are not contradictory: agent 02 is right that $6.1B sitting unused while shares dilute is a per-share governance flaw; agent 01 is right that management's overall delivery record is strong. The synthesis preserves both scores — capital allocation at 73 (reflecting the buyback blemish with a factual red flag) and management quality at 88 (reflecting the broader track record). The more conservative read on the buyback is maintained.

## 5. Stewardship Summary

Amazon's management, led by Andy Jassy (CEO, 5-year tenure, 28 years at Amazon) and Brian Olsavsky (CFO, 11 years), has delivered on its promises across a three-year window: operating income grew 6.5x, AWS re-accelerated from 13% to 28% growth, International turned profitable ($4.8B operating income in FY2025), and the FCF compression from the AI capex cycle was explicitly pre-warned in the FY2024 shareholder letter and then delivered exactly as telegraphed. These are actions and numbers from audited filings — not narrative. The capital record is strong on organic reinvestment (ROIC 18–20%, incremental ROIC 35% at its peak) but carries a real blemish: $6.1B of a $10B buyback authorization has been dormant for three years while RSU vesting dilutes shares at ~1.7%/year, and the only buyback executed ($6B in FY2022) was not done at the price trough. Management's answer — that reinvesting in AWS at 18–20% ROIC beats repurchasing at a market multiple — is internally consistent, but it leaves per-share value creation entirely dependent on earnings growth, with no share-count reduction acting as a safety valve. Incentives are directionally right (equity-only, no annual bonus, no size-based metrics, a 10-year back-loaded vesting schedule) but structurally unusual in that there is no performance condition on RSU vesting — time served is sufficient — which means the structure does not structurally penalize stagnation, even though it has rewarded performance during Jassy's tenure. Minority shareholders have structurally sound rights: no dual class, no staggered board, no poison pill, majority voting, annual elections, and proxy access. The one trust signal worth weighing most carefully is the management team's behavior when results fell short of expectations: in both Q3 and Q4 2025, charges were named, sized, and attributed in the opening sentences of prepared remarks, not buried — which is how candid operators communicate. The single most important reason to trust this team with capital is that combination of the promise-versus-delivery record and the transparent handling of a difficult FCF story.

## 5D. Analyst Follow-Up Questions

**RF-CAP-002 (Buyback dormancy / share dilution):**
1. Does management have a stated trigger price or ROIC threshold at which it would activate the $6.1B buyback authorization? Or will it lapse?
2. Given FCF is guided to recover in FY2027–2028 as AI capacity monetizes, is there a committed buyback plan once CFO exceeds a threshold (e.g., $120B)?
3. Why was the authorization dormant specifically in FY2023 when the stock traded at $90–$153 — well below the $130 average buyback price in FY2022?

**RF-SHR-001 (Say-on-pay historical opposition):**
1. The improvement from 22% to ~6% opposition at the 2026 meeting — was this structural (e.g., proxy advisory firm recommendation change) or a function of strong operating income / stock performance masking the same structural concern about no performance conditions?
2. When the 2021 CEO RSU grant fully vests in 2031 and a new CEO grant is issued, will the Compensation Committee add explicit ROIC or TSR performance conditions?

**RF-DISC-002 (borderline — recurring restructuring):**
1. The ~$700M–$800M annual "other operating expense" baseline has been described as non-recurring in each of FY2023, FY2024, and FY2025. Will management provide a "normalized operating cost" baseline that acknowledges the structural annual charge?
2. What is the net multi-year P&L impact of the server useful-life changes (5→6yr in FY2024, 6→5yr in FY2025) on a consolidated basis? Is another change expected in FY2026?

**AI revenue transparency (Amber finding 06-011):**
1. Microsoft discloses Azure AI revenue; Google discloses Cloud AI revenue. Given Amazon's $200B FY2026 capex is predominantly AI-justified, will AWS begin disclosing a separate AI revenue or bookings metric within the AWS segment?
2. What is the current annual run rate for AWS generative AI revenue (Bedrock, SageMaker AI, Trainium/Inferentia capacity)? The $364B AWS backlog is a total figure — what portion is specifically AI-workload contracted?

**Board tenure (Amber finding 05-006):**
1. Patricia Q. Stonesifer has served on the Board since February 1997 — 29 years. She chairs the Compensation Committee. Has the Nominating and Corporate Governance Committee evaluated her continued independence from management given her tenure predates all current executive leadership by many years?

## 6. What Would Change The Stewardship Verdict?

| Current Verdict | What Would Strengthen It | What Would Weaken It | Data Needed |
|---|---|---|---|
| Aligned & competent | (1) Add explicit ROIC or TSR performance condition to RSU grants in the next CEO compensation package; (2) activate the buyback authorization in FY2027 once FCF recovers, reducing net share count; (3) FY2026 incremental ROIC holds at ≥15%, confirming the AI capex thesis; (4) provide AI-specific revenue disclosure within AWS | (1) FY2026 incremental ROIC falls below 12%, indicating the AI capex bet is over-earning its cost; (2) management changes KPIs or guidance window after the capex cycle underperforms (would trigger RF-MGT-003); (3) large debt-funded acquisition of an unrelated business while AI FCF is negative (would approach RF-CAP-004); (4) Bezos significantly accelerates stake sales (e.g., below 5%) before any operating inflection | (1) 2026 DEF 14A proxy statement — would lift incentive alignment cap from 50 and confirm board composition details; (2) Form 4 insider transaction log for the last 12 months — would confirm timing and scale of all Bezos sales; (3) FY2026 annual operating results — incremental ROIC on the $200B capex year is the single most important forward data point |

## 7. Note To The Final Synthesizer

- **Stewardship verdict: Aligned & competent.** The single strongest piece of evidence is that operating income grew 6.5x from $12.2B (FY2022) to $80.0B (FY2025) and six of six multi-year checkable promises were kept or exceeded — including AWS re-acceleration from 13% to 28%, International segment profitability, and the FCF compression that was explicitly pre-warned. These are audited filing numbers, not narrative.

- **Capital allocation record: Value-creative.** ROIC ran at 18–20% in FY2024–FY2025 (above an estimated ~10% cost of capital) and incremental ROIC peaked at 35% in FY2023. The blemish is the dormant buyback: $6.1B of a $10B authorization has been unused for three years while RSU vesting dilutes shares at ~1.7%/year. Per-share CFO tripled from $4.52 (FY2021) to $12.97 (FY2025) despite 5% dilution — value is being created, but it could be distributed more efficiently. The $200B FY2026 capex plan is the dominant open question on capital allocation: if incremental ROIC falls below 12% as a result, the thesis weakens.

- **Incentive and ownership alignment:** Directionally aligned. No annual bonus, no size-based metrics, 10-year back-loaded RSU vesting (>80% vesting in years 6–10) — all of which remove short-term earnings management pressure. The structural limitation is the absence of any performance condition on RSU vesting: time served is sufficient to vest. Bezos's ~8.8% founder equity (not grants) is the deepest alignment signal in the structure; all other officers hold only grant-origin RSUs with no open-market purchases. The incentive cap (max 50) applies because the 2026 DEF 14A proxy — which would confirm exact compensation metrics, weights, and AGM voting details — is absent from the pool.

- **Biggest governance risk:** RF-CAP-002 (buyback dormancy / net share dilution) — High severity, not Critical. The $6.1B authorization unused while ~558M shares were issued via SBC is a structural per-share dilution flaw, not a control-abuse or fraud concern. The second-largest risk is RF-SHR-001 (persistent 22% say-on-pay opposition at the 2024 and 2025 meetings), which has improved materially in 2026 (~6% against) but whose structural cause (no performance conditions on RSU grants) has not been addressed.

- **Hard disqualifier from business-model/01:** **None flagged.** All eight disqualifiers clear: unqualified audit opinion, no pledging, no material RPTs, no auditor changes, no restatement, no enforcement action on financial reporting, no customer concentration, and positive operating cash flow in all four years reviewed.

- **§24 rejector filters:** All six filters assessed and none triggered. Specifically: (1) No integrity signal (RF-MGT-005 not emitted); (2) Not a turnaround (RF-MGT-004 not applicable); (3) No high-debt concern (net debt/EBITDA 0.38x, EBIT/interest 35x); (4) No serial-acquirer pattern (RF-CAP-004 not triggered — only two material deals over five years at <3% of CFO); (5) No fast-changing industry cap applied; (6) No structurally unaligned controlling owner (RF-OWN-004 not triggered — Bezos's objectives are aligned with per-share value, no dual class, no parent extracting value).

- **Partial-data caps applied:** (1) Incentive alignment capped at 50 (proxy absent); (2) Shareholder friendliness capped at 60 (no complete beneficial ownership table or Form 4 log in pool); (3) Overall usefulness capped at 70 (proxy absent). These caps reduce the Confidence-Adjusted Governance Score from what would be ~83 (if proxy data were available) to **54**.

- **Biggest missing data point (single highest-value next data request):** The 2026 DEF 14A proxy statement. This single document would supply: the Compensation Discussion and Analysis (exact performance metrics and weights — or confirmation there are none — for all NEO plans), the beneficial ownership table (exact percentage holdings for Bezos, Jassy, and all institutional holders ≥5%), the full board independence table and committee composition, the AGM voting results (say-on-pay, director elections, shareholder proposals), auditor fee analysis (audit vs non-audit), and the RPT table (Item 13). It would lift both the incentive alignment cap (from 50 to likely 60–65) and the shareholder friendliness cap (from 60 to likely 75–80), and would increase the Confidence-Adjusted Governance Score from 54 to approximately 65–70.

- **Explicit handoff:** This module supersedes the `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance` quick-read. The master synthesizer should treat this module's governance verdict (Aligned & competent), Governance Score (72/100), Confidence-Adjusted Score (54/100), Governance Rating (Good), and red-flag register (RF-CAP-002 High, RF-SHR-001 High, zero Critical) as the primary governance read. The `business-model/11` quick-read score (62/100) was a preliminary pass and is now superseded.

---

## 8. Simple Summary

- **Has management delivered on its promises?** Yes — six of six multi-year checkable promises (AWS re-acceleration, International profitability, cost-to-serve reduction, operating income recovery, FCF compression pre-warning) were kept or exceeded over FY2023–FY2025. Revenue beat in every reported quarter. Q3 and Q4 2025 charges were owned plainly in opening sentences of prepared remarks, not buried.

- **Has capital created or destroyed per-share value?** Created, but with a dilution drag. CFO per share tripled from $4.52 to $12.97 over five years; ROIC ran at 18–20% (above cost of capital). The drag is a $6.1B buyback dormancy alongside ~$20B annual RSU issuance — shares outstanding grew 5.0% over five years without buyback offset. The $200B FY2026 AI capex plan is the unresolved binary: if incremental ROIC stays above 12%, capital allocation remains value-creative; if it falls below, the thesis weakens.

- **What does the pay actually reward?** Time served. Jassy's $212M RSU grant (July 2021) vests on a 10-year back-loaded schedule regardless of operating performance — there is no ROIC threshold, no TSR hurdle, and no EPS condition. The link to per-share value is through the stock price (which has risen 4.1x since Jassy's appointment), but the vesting itself is unconditional. The structure removes short-term earnings-management incentives (no annual bonus, no size-based metrics) but also removes any penalty for mediocre performance.

- **How much skin in the game do insiders have, and are they buying or selling?** Bezos holds ~8.8% (pure founder equity — the highest-quality form). All other officers hold RSU grants only; no named insider has made an open-market purchase. Net activity is selling, but all sales are under pre-set 10b5-1(c) plans adopted in November 2025; Bezos's July 2025 selling preceded strong quarterly results. No adverse timing. No pledging.

- **Are minority shareholders protected?** Structurally yes. No dual-class shares, no staggered board, no poison pill, annual director elections by majority vote, proxy access, 25% special-meeting threshold. The single friction point is the persistent institutional opposition to the compensation structure (22% say-on-pay opposition at the 2024 and 2025 meetings), which improved to ~6% in 2026 — the improvement is real but warrants monitoring when the next large CEO grant is issued.

- **Is management candid when results are bad?** Yes. Charges in Q3 2025 ($4.3B) and Q4 2025 ($2.4B) were disclosed in the opening paragraph of prepared remarks with dollar amounts, line-item attribution, and segment impact. FCF compression was pre-warned in writing before it occurred. There is no history of guidance set-to-miss being obscured.

- **Any hard disqualifier flagged?** No. All eight disqualifiers clear. No verdict lock.

- **Is this module useful for the master synthesizer?** Yes, at 70/100 usefulness (capped from the proxy absence). The management quality, capital allocation, and candor reads are high-confidence. The incentive alignment and shareholder-friendliness reads are partial-confidence. The single most important upgrade would be adding the 2026 DEF 14A to the data pool.

## 3. Governance Usability Check

| Requirement | Available? (Y/N) | Source | Why It Matters |
|---|---|---|---|
| Proxy / DEF 14A | **N** | Not in pool. Both the 2025 proxy (relating to the 2025 Annual Meeting, deferred from FY2024 10-K) and the 2026 proxy (relating to the 2026 Annual Meeting, deferred from FY2025 10-K) are absent. | Compensation detail, beneficial ownership table, board independence, related-party disclosure, auditor fees, and AGM voting outcomes all live in the proxy. |
| Compensation disclosure (metrics/weights) | **Partial** | FY2025 10-K (Apr 9, 2026) defers Items 11–14 to the 2026 proxy. The Capital IQ Takeover Defenses RTF states that compensation is tied to long-term performance "reflected primarily in the stock price" and notes RSUs as the primary vehicle. 10-K Item 9B discloses specific 10b5-1 plan adoptions (Bezos: 15M shares through Feb 2026; Jassy: 142,224 shares through Dec 2026). Stock-based compensation expense is disclosed ($24.0B in FY2025, $22.0B in FY2024, $19.5B in FY2023). However, the actual bonus metrics, weightings, and thresholds for the Leadership Development and Compensation Committee are not in the pool. | Incentive alignment — the critical question of whether comp rewards per-share value or revenue/size cannot be answered without the proxy's Compensation Discussion & Analysis. |
| Beneficial ownership table | **Partial** | Capital IQ Public Company Profile lists Bezos (Founder & Executive Chairman) and Jassy (President, CEO & Director) by name and role; float is 90.9% (as of Jul 1, 2026). Bezos's approximate 9–10% ownership and Jassy's much smaller stake are widely reported and referenced in the business-model module. However, no formal Schedule 13D/13G, no proxy beneficial ownership table (5%+ holders), and no Form 4 export is present in the pool. Ownership figures for specific holders cannot be cited to a filing in the pool. | Skin in the game, concentration of voting power, and whether controlling insiders bought or were granted their shares. |
| Insider-transaction data (buys/sells) | **Partial** | FY2025 10-K Item 9B discloses that Bezos adopted a 10b5-1 trading plan in Nov 2025 (up to 15M shares through Feb 2026) and Jassy adopted a plan in Nov 2025 (up to 142,224 shares through Dec 2026). Q4 2025 transcript and 10-K note Olsavsky terminated a prior plan. No comprehensive Form 4 transaction log or Capital IQ insider-transaction export is present. | Conviction signal — planned sales vs distress-driven sales, and net insider buying/selling over 12 months. |
| Board composition / independence | **Partial** | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses RTF states: one-year board terms (not staggered), majority voting, lead independent director provision, no classified board, no cumulative voting, 90-day advance notice for nominations, CEO succession plan exists, board performance reviews occur. FY2025 10-K signatures page lists 13 directors (Jeff Bezos — Executive Chairman; Andy Jassy — CEO/Director; 11 others). Capital IQ Public Company Profile lists Cooper (Edith W.) as Independent Director. The full board independence breakdown, committee assignments, and number of independent directors on each committee (audit, comp, nominating) are in the absent 2026 proxy. | Board quality and entrenchment risk. |
| Related-party disclosure | **Partial** | FY2025 10-K Item 13 states: "Information required by Item 13 of Part III is included in our Proxy Statement relating to our 2026 Annual Meeting of Shareholders and is incorporated herein by reference." The FY2025 10-K financial notes contain no RPT note — and explicitly state no internal revenue transactions between segments. Disqualifier scan confirmed no material RPT. | Value leakage and conflict of interest. Proxy has the full RPT table. |
| Control structure (dual-class / blocs) | **Y** | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses RTF: "Dual Class Stock" field is listed but value is blank in the extract, suggesting no dual class. Public Company Profile confirms float 90.9%, single-class common stock. FY2025 10-K cover page: one class of common stock listed on NASDAQ. Takeover Defenses export states "Active Poison Pill" — value appears blank/inapplicable. No super-voting or dual-class structure is present. Bezos's control is through a large but not dominant economic interest (~9–10%), not a structural voting bloc. | Minority-shareholder rights and control abuse risk. |
| Prior shareholder letters / guidance | **Y** | Three consecutive annual shareholder letters in pool: Andy Jassy's FY2025 letter (filed Apr 9, 2026), FY2024 letter (filed Feb 6, 2025), and FY2023 letter (filed Feb 1, 2024). The 1997 Jeff Bezos letter reprinted in each 10-K provides a philosophical baseline. Five earnings call transcripts cover Q2 2025 through Q1 2026. Capital IQ Guidance tab and Surprise tab provide historical guidance-vs-actual data. | Promise-vs-delivery testing — the core management track record check. |
| M&A / buyback / dividend history | **Y** | FY2023, FY2024, FY2025 10-K cash flow statements and notes; Capital IQ Financials (Cash Flow, Capital Structure) cover FY2020–Q1 2026. Business-model module `11_capital-allocation-governance.md` has already extracted the multi-year capital allocation scorecard. ## 6. Sufficiency Verdict

- **Verdict: Partial**

- **Reason:** The three most recent annual 10-Ks, the Q1 2026 10-Q, five earnings transcripts, three shareholder letters, and rich Capital IQ financials / governance exports are in the pool and support a thorough read of management track record, capital allocation, and disclosure candor — but both the 2025 and 2026 DEF 14A proxy statements are absent, leaving compensation discussion and analysis (exact metrics, weightings, thresholds), the formal beneficial ownership table (exact percentage holdings for named insiders and 5%+ institutional holders), committee-level board independence, AGM voting outcomes, and the full RPT table either missing or only partially reconstructible from other sources.

- **Specialists that can run:**
  - Management track record (agent 01) — **Yes, fully**; multi-year 10-K history, shareholder letters, transcripts, cross-module `01_disqualifier-scan` and `04_guidance-consensus` all available.
  - Capital allocation scorecard (agent 02) — **Yes, fully**; three years of 10-K cash flows, Capital IQ financials, and cross-module `11_capital-allocation-governance` provide a complete multi-year picture.
  - Incentives and compensation (agent 03) — **Partial**; can assess the structure (RSU-dominant, no options, high SBC quantum) and the 10b5-1 plan disclosures, but cannot state the actual performance metrics or weightings without the proxy. Hard cap applies.
  - Ownership and insider behavior (agent 04) — **Partial**; named insiders and float are available; 10b5-1 plan activity is disclosed in the 10-K; formal ownership percentages from the proxy are absent. Cap applies but meaningful inference is possible.
  - Board and shareholder rights (agent 05) — **Partial**; board structure (13 directors, 1-year terms, majority voting, no classified board, no dual class, lead independent director) is available from Capital IQ and 10-K; full independence breakdown and committee composition require the absent proxy. Cap applies.
  - Candor and disclosure quality (agent 06) — **Yes, largely**; five transcripts, three shareholder letters, historical beat/miss data (Capital IQ Surprise tab), earnings-quality cross-module, and guidance-consensus cross-module all available.

- **Hard disqualifier already flagged by `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan`?** **No.** All 8 disqualifiers clear. No verdict lock. The disqualifier scan verdict is: "No disqualifier triggered. Proceed to deeper analysis."

- **Active partial-data caps:**
  - Incentive alignment score: **max 50** (no proxy / compensation disclosure)
  - Overall usefulness score: **max 70** (no proxy / compensation disclosure)
  - Shareholder friendliness score: **max 60** (no complete ownership / insider-transaction data)
  - Board-independence sub-dimension: **not fully assessable** — cap board quality at 60 for the independence-and-committee dimension specifically; the structural governance read (no dual class, no staggered board, majority voting, annual terms) is assessable and positive

- **Critical missing items:**
  - 2026 DEF 14A proxy statement (relates to 2025 fiscal year, filing due within 120 days of Dec 31, 2025 — expected by Apr 29, 2026; likely filed by now on EDGAR but not in the data pool)
  - 2025 DEF 14A proxy statement (relates to 2024 fiscal year)
  - Form 4 insider-transaction log (complete 12-month buy/sell history for named insiders)
  - Beneficial ownership table (exact % holdings for Bezos, Jassy, and institutional holders ≥5%)
  - AGM voting results (votes for/against on compensation, director elections, shareholder proposals)

- **Single highest-value missing document:** 2026 DEF 14A proxy statement — it would supply compensation metrics and weightings, the beneficial ownership table, board independence breakdown, committee composition, AGM voting results, auditor fee analysis, and the RPT table in a single document.

# Management & Track Record — AMZN

**Reporting standard:** US GAAP. **Currency:** USD. **Fiscal year end:** December 31. **Filing regime:** US SEC. **As of:** 2026-07-04.

## 1. Key Executives

| President & CEO | Andrew R. (Andy) Jassy | July 2021 | ~5 years | Joined Amazon May 1997; built and led AWS from inception, serving as SVP Amazon Web Services Apr 2006–Apr 2016 and CEO Amazon Web Services Apr 2016–Jul 2021; Harvard MBA; 28+ years at Amazon | FY2025 10-K, p.4–5 (Executive Officers section); Web: Wikipedia / Amazon SEC filings (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Senior VP & CFO | Brian T. Olsavsky | June 2015 | ~11 years | Joined Amazon Apr 2002; VP Finance Global Consumer Business Dec 2011–Jun 2015; numerous financial leadership roles across Amazon globally since 2002 | FY2025 10-K, p.4–5 (Executive Officers section) |
| CEO, Amazon Web Services | Matthew S. Garman | June 2024 | ~1 year | SVP Amazon Web Services Feb 2021–Jun 2024; VP Marketing, Sales and Support Amazon Web Services 2013–2021 | FY2025 10-K, p.4–5 (Executive Officers section) |
| CEO, Worldwide Amazon Stores | Douglas J. Herrington | July 2022 | ~3 years | SVP North America Consumer Jan 2015–Jul 2022; VP Consumables May 2005–Dec 2014 | FY2025 10-K, p.4–5 (Executive Officers section) |
| Executive Chair (founder) | Jeffrey P. Bezos | July 2021 (chair since 1994) | Founder / 32 years | Founded Amazon 1994; CEO May 1996–Jul 2021; Board Chair since 1994 | FY2025 10-K, p.4–5 (Executive Officers section) |
| VP Worldwide Controller & Principal Accounting Officer | Shelley L. Reynolds | April 2007 | ~19 years | Controller role only; VP role since Apr 2007 | FY2025 10-K, p.4–5 (Executive Officers section) |
| SVP, Chief Global Affairs & Legal Officer | David A. Zapolsky | February 2025 (current role; at Amazon since 2012) | ~1 year in current role | General Counsel / SVP since 2012; Secretary Sep 2012–Jan 2024 | FY2025 10-K, p.4–5 (Executive Officers section) |

**Founder / owner-operator status:** Jeff Bezos is the founder and holds approximately 8.8% of shares outstanding as of the 2026 proxy statement (950 million shares; web-sourced, unverified — the proxy is not in the data pool but the estimate is consistent with the FY2025 10-K Item 9B disclosure of a Nov 2025 10b5-1 plan to sell up to 15 million shares through Feb 2027). Bezos is Executive Chair, not CEO. Andy Jassy is a professional manager with long Amazon tenure, not a founder. Jassy holds a small equity position through RSU vesting; shares sold via a pre-set 10b5-1 plan adopted Nov 2025 to sell up to 142,224 shares through Dec 2026 — a modest transaction against a large share count and not a distress signal. Amazon is best described as a **founder-chaired, professional-CEO-run company**.

---

## 2. Management Stability

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Senior departures (last 3 years) | No CEO or CFO departure in the last 3 years. Matthew Garman was promoted to CEO of AWS in June 2024 (successor to Adam Selipsky, who stepped down — internal succession). David Zapolsky moved from General Counsel to new Chief Global Affairs & Legal Officer role Feb 2025 — an expansion, not a departure. No unexplained exits at the C-suite level. | FY2025 10-K, p.4–5; capital-allocation-governance cross-module output |
| CFO turnover | Zero. Brian Olsavsky has been CFO since June 2015 — 11 years of continuous tenure. He terminated a prior 10b5-1 trading plan in November 2025, which is routine and does not signal resignation or distress. | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 |
| CEO succession / continuity | Andy Jassy has been CEO since July 2021. The Bezos → Jassy transition in July 2021 was planned and deliberate — Bezos had signaled his intent to step into the Executive Chair role publicly before it occurred, and Jassy was publicly identified as the successor. No emergency succession occurred. | FY2025 10-K, p.4–5; Web: Amazon SEC Form 8-K July 2021 (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Board chair | Jeff Bezos has been Board Chair continuously since 1994, now in the Executive Chair role since July 2021 — unchanged. | FY2025 10-K, p.4–5 |
| AWS leadership succession | Adam Selipsky stepped down from AWS CEO in June 2024; Matt Garman, an internal candidate with 10+ years in senior AWS roles, was immediately promoted. No external hire was needed; continuity maintained. | FY2025 10-K, p.4–5 |

**Stability assessment:** The senior team is the most stable it has been in years. Three consecutive years (2023–2025) with no CEO, CFO, or Chair change. The AWS CEO change in mid-2024 was orderly internal succession, not an unexplained departure. No CFO turnover — the single most reliable warning signal for governance deterioration — is a clear positive. No warnings triggered here.

---

## 3. Stated Strategy & Targets

Management's stated strategy, as set out across the FY2025 Shareholder Letter (Andy Jassy, Apr 2026), the FY2024 Shareholder Letter (Andy Jassy, Apr 2025), and the FY2025 10-K, rests on three explicit bets: (1) continuing to lower the cost of and accelerate delivery speed in the Stores/retail business, targeting grocery and same-day delivery as the next expansion layer, with an explicit investment of over $4 billion in rural delivery and plans to reach 1 billion additional packages per year to 13,000 zip codes; (2) investing aggressively in AWS and AI infrastructure — approximately $200 billion of capital expenditures in 2026 — to capitalize on enterprise cloud migration and generative AI demand, with management explicitly stating they are "investing to be the meaningful leader" and citing the AWS AI revenue run rate exceeding $15 billion annually in Q1 2026; and (3) building new long-cycle businesses (Amazon Leo satellite broadband, Project Kuiper, Zoox autonomous vehicles, Amazon Pharmacy, robotics) where management explicitly accepts near-term FCF compression in exchange for what it describes as large long-term ROIC. The explicit, checkable targets management has set are: AWS growth continuing above 20% year-over-year (delivered 20% in FY2025 and 28% in Q1 2026); operating margin improvement toward and beyond 11% (delivered 11.2% in FY2025); reduction in cost-to-serve per unit (delivered in 2023 for the first time since 2018, continued in 2024); and FCF recovering after the capex surge once capacity is monetized — though management has not set a specific FCF dollar target for the recovery.

---

The Q1 2026 EPS beat of 69.5% is partly inflated by Anthropic mark-to-market gains (as flagged in `earnings/06_earnings-quality` and `business-model/12_red-flags-sweep`); normalized EPS excluded those investment gains and still reflected strong operating outperformance. Guidance has consistently been set conservatively, particularly for operating income.

## 4A. Turnaround & Integrity Tests (CLAUDE.md §24, Filters 1 & 2)

| Test | Finding | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Is the thesis a turnaround (struggling business + new management / new plan)? | **No.** Amazon is not a turnaround. The business was generating positive operating cash flow in all four most-recent years and positive (and growing) operating income since FY2023. The Jassy era involved a restructuring (2022–2023 layoffs, cost-to-serve reduction) after an over-expansion in 2021, but the company never approached distress. The "inflection" from operating income of $12.2B (FY2022) to $80B (FY2025) is real and delivered — this is a recovery from an investment overcycle, not a turnaround of a broken business. | FY2022–FY2025 10-K; Capital IQ Financials — Income Statement tab |
| If yes: years of proven operating inflection delivered | N/A — not a turnaround. | — |
| Résumé / plan / consultant deck cited as the reason to believe? | **No.** The bull case rests on delivered operating income growth (6.5x from FY2022 to FY2025), real AWS revenue acceleration (13% → 20% → 28%), and confirmed customer commitments ($364B AWS backlog at Q1 2026 close). These are filing-level facts, not narrative. | FY2025 10-K; Q1 2026 10-Q; Q1 2026 Earnings Call transcript |
| Routed integrity "buzz" from `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan` | **No adverse integrity signal routed.** The disqualifier scan found zero disqualifiers triggered, no integrity flag, no unverified adverse signal. The only regulatory matter (FTC settlement, $2.5B, Q3 2025) concerned marketplace conduct, was settled and disclosed fully, and did not implicate financial reporting integrity. | business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md (all 8 disqualifiers: N) |

## 5. Management Read

Andy Jassy has been CEO for five years and has delivered substantially on what he promised: the 2022–2023 efficiency restructuring translated into operating income growth from $12.2B (FY2022) to $80.0B (FY2025) — a 6.5x increase — while AWS has re-accelerated from 13% to 28% growth exactly as management telegraphed. The CFO (Olsavsky, 11-year tenure) and team have set and met guidance consistently, and the one material miss relative to investor expectations — free cash flow compression in FY2025 — was explicitly warned in advance in the FY2024 shareholder letter and is management-chosen rather than a surprise. The single biggest management signal, positive in nature, is the combination of deep institutional knowledge (Jassy spent 25+ years at Amazon before becoming CEO, building AWS from scratch), a record of set-and-meet promises over a multi-year window, and stable leadership depth (no CEO/CFO changes in three years, orderly AWS succession). The primary uncertainty is not management competence but the binary risk of the $200B annual capex bet on AI infrastructure — a bet management is making consciously and with disclosed customer pre-commitments, but one where execution risk and demand-realization risk are real.

## management-governance / 02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md

_Source: `02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md`_

# Capital-Allocation Scorecard — AMZN

**Reporting currency:** USD. **Standard:** US GAAP. **Fiscal year:** ends December 31. **Period covered:** FY2021–FY2025 (five years). **Jurisdiction:** US / SEC. **Filing regime:** 10-K annual, 10-Q quarterly.

**Cross-module inputs used:** `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md`, `earnings/01_historical-financials.md`, `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md`. Upstream `01_management-and-track-record.md` is present in the output directory and incorporated.

---

## 1. Uses of Capital (FY2021–FY2025)

All figures in USD millions. CFO denominator = $433,416M (five-year sum). Figures sourced from Capital IQ Cash Flow export (FY2021–FY2025) and corroborated by 10-K filings where noted.

| Use | FY2021 | FY2022 | FY2023 | FY2024 | FY2025 | Cumulative 5-Yr | % of 5-Yr CFO | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|
| Capex (gross, incl. leasehold improvements) | $61,053 | $63,645 | $52,729 | $82,999 | $131,819 | $392,245 | 90.5% | Capital IQ Cash Flow; FY2025 10-K, p.36 CF Statement |
| Cash acquisitions (incl. equity investments) | $1,985 | $8,316 | $5,839 | $7,082 | $3,841 | $27,063 | 6.2% | Capital IQ Cash Flow; FY2025 10-K, Note 5, p.55 |
| Buybacks | $0 | $6,000 | $0 | $0 | $0 | $6,000 | 1.4% | FY2025 10-K, Note 8 (Stock Repurchase Activity), p.62 |
| Dividends | $0 | $0 | $0 | $0 | $0 | $0 | 0.0% | FY2025 10-K, Item 5, p.19 |
| Net debt repayment (gross issued less repaid) | ($6,291)* | ($15,718) | $16,129 | $11,812 | ($9,661) | ($3,729) | (0.9%) | Capital IQ Cash Flow (financing activities) |
| Stock-based compensation (non-cash dilution proxy) | $12,757 | $19,621 | $24,023 | $22,011 | $19,467 | $97,879 | 22.6% | Capital IQ Supplemental; FY2025 10-K, Note 8, p.62 |

\* Negative = net debt issuance (more issued than repaid) for that year; FY2021 financing CF was +$6.3B net inflow.

**Five-year operating cash flow (CFO):** $433,416M (accelerating from $46.3B in FY2021 to $139.5B in FY2025; +201% growth). [Capital IQ Cash Flow; FY2025 10-K, p.36]

**The dominant story in one line:** Capex absorbs 90.5 cents of every dollar of CFO generated over five years. Everything else is rounding noise by comparison.

---

## 2. M&A Scorecard

| Deal | Year | Price (net of cash) | Disclosed Rationale | Synergy / Return Delivered? | Opportunity Cost | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|---|---|
| MGM Holdings Inc. | FY2022 | $6.1B cash + assumed $2.5B debt repaid = $8.6B economic | Content library (4,000+ films, 17,000+ TV shows) to strengthen Prime Video; compete with Netflix/Disney | Partially delivered: Prime Video audience grew to 315M average ad-supported viewers by Q1 2026 (up from 200M in early 2024); Prime Video advertising "contributing meaningfully to revenue growth." No standalone financial disclosure; returns not separately measurable. Goodwill in NA segment: $22.8B consolidated (FY2023), reflecting cumulative acquisitions not just MGM. | Low direct opportunity cost: debt assumed was repaid immediately; deal was small vs company scale (~0.6% of 2022 revenue base). Content is a durable strategic asset. | FY2025 10-K, Note 5, p.55; Q1 2026 Earnings Call, Apr 29, 2026; FY2024 10-K, Note 5, p.56 |
| 1Life Healthcare (One Medical) | FY2023 | $3.5B cash (~$3.9B gross) | Primary care clinics (~200 locations, 815,000 members); expand Amazon into healthcare | Functionally delivered: By Q1 2026, One Medical underpins "Health AI," Amazon's 24/7 AI-powered health agent (Q1 2026 Earnings Call, Apr 29, 2026); no disclosed standalone revenue or margin. Integration appears operational (Health AI launched with One Medical clinicians). Long-term financial returns not determinable from available data. | Moderate: healthcare is far from Amazon's core; integration risk is real. No evidence of forced divestitures or franchise distraction. No synergy figures disclosed. | FY2025 10-K, Note 5, p.55; Q1 2026 Earnings Call transcript, Apr 29, 2026 |
| Bolt-on acquisitions (FY2024) | FY2024 | $780M aggregate | Technology and know-how to serve customers; expand customer base | Not separately disclosed; immaterial individually; pro forma disclosure waived | Immaterial | FY2025 10-K, Note 5, p.55 |
| Bolt-on / immaterial (FY2025) | FY2025 | Immaterial (not quantified) | Same rationale as FY2024 | Not separately disclosed | Immaterial | FY2025 10-K, Note 5, p.55 |
| Anthropic (equity investment, minority stake) | FY2023–2025 | ~$8.0B cumulative ($1.25B FY2023; $4.05B FY2024; $2.7B FY2025) | Strategic AI investment; cloud preference agreement; supports AWS AI capabilities | Mark-to-market gains: fair value of convertible notes ~$13.8B as of Dec 31, 2024 (on $5.3B cost basis). FY2025 upward adjustment from equity investments (primarily Anthropic): $7.7B unrealized gain. Returns are currently paper gains on a private company; cash realization is not certain. | Not an acquisition (minority note/equity stake, not control). No divestitures forced. | FY2025 10-K, p.146 (MD&A), p.212 (Note re equity investments); FY2024 10-K, Note re Anthropic investments |

**Serial-acquirer test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 4):** Amazon is NOT a serial acquirer. Over five years, it completed two material transactions (MGM and One Medical) totalling ~$9.6B in acquisition consideration — less than 3% of cumulative CFO and well under 1% of the company's market value at each deal date. No deal was debt-funded. No divestiture was forced to fund an acquisition. There is no pattern of multiple debt-funded transformational bets. RF-CAP-004 is NOT triggered. The M&A discipline component is not capped.

**Opportunity cost verdict:** Neither MGM nor One Medical forced a divestiture or demonstrably pulled management focus from the core AWS/retail franchise. One Medical has been integrated into Amazon's Health AI product, suggesting strategic embed rather than standalone dilution. Returns on MGM are strategically visible (streaming audience growth, ad revenue) but not financially disclosed in segmented form. Returns on One Medical are not yet financially measurable. Based on available evidence, both deals appear rationally priced capability purchases, not empire-building — consistent with the §3 conservative default applied to non-disclosed returns.

---

## 3. Buyback Scorecard

| Year | Amount Spent | Currency | Shares Bought | Implied Avg Price | Share-Count Change (YoY, M shares) | Offset SBC Dilution? | Price-Discipline Evidence | Judgment | Source |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---|---|---|
| FY2021 | $0 | USD | 0 | N/A | +62M net dilution | No | $10B program not yet authorized | No buybacks | FY2023 10-K, Note 8 |
| FY2022 | $6,000 | USD | 46.2M | ~$129.87 | +141M net dilution despite buyback | No (141M net dilution = ~556M SBC-issued minus 46.2M repurchased) | Stock 52-wk range 2022: ~$102–$188 (post-split). Buybacks concentrated in H1 2022 before stock fell to $102 in Nov 2022. Bought at ~$130, near mid-range — not at the bottom. | Partial — buyback executed but at a sub-optimal price and did not reduce net share count | FY2024 10-K, Note 8; FY2023 10-K, Note 8 (Stock Repurchase Activity) |
| FY2023 | $0 | USD | 0 | N/A | +210M net dilution | No | $6.1B authorization unused; stock ranged ~$90–$153 (post-split; significant undervaluation by late 2022 / early 2023 standards) | No buybacks when arguably the best opportunity existed | FY2025 10-K, Note 8, p.62 |
| FY2024 | $0 | USD | 0 | N/A | +138M net dilution | No | $6.1B authorization unused; stock recovered $153–$225 | No buybacks as stock rerated | FY2025 10-K, Note 8, p.62 |
| FY2025 | $0 | USD | 0 | N/A | +23M net dilution | No | $6.1B authorization unused; stock $180–$244 | No buybacks; program fully dormant | FY2025 10-K, Note 8, p.62 |

**Key findings:**

1. **The $10B buyback program authorized in March 2022 has been 39% used (only $3.9B) in four years.** $6.1B remains unused as of December 31, 2025. [FY2025 10-K, Note 8, p.62]

2. **Buybacks never offset SBC dilution.** Shares outstanding rose from 10,242M (FY2021) to 10,754M (FY2025), a net increase of 512M shares (+5.0% over five years or ~+1.0%/year). RF-CAP-002 is triggered (buybacks not reducing share count). Gross SBC vesting over the period was approximately 558M shares; the $6B buyback offset only 46.2M of those shares in one year.

3. **Intrinsic-value discipline not fully assessable from this module** (valuation module has not run). However, on observable evidence: the $129.87/share average buyback price in FY2022 was not at the post-split trough (~$102 in late 2022). The company bought $6B before the stock fell and did nothing as the stock spent much of FY2023 well below the buyback price. This is not a pattern of patient, opportunistic repurchasing.

4. **Insiders sold into the dormant buyback period.** Jeff Bezos adopted a Rule 10b5-1 plan to sell up to 15M shares through February 2026; Andy Jassy adopted a plan to sell up to 142,224 shares through December 2026. The company was simultaneously sitting on $6.1B in unused buyback authorization. [FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74]

5. **Counterpoint:** Management's stated rationale for not buying back stock — that all capital should be reinvested in AWS/AI infrastructure — is internally consistent. The $131.8B capex in FY2025 dwarfs the $6.1B buyback authorization by 21x. The decision not to buy back shares is a defensible opportunity-cost trade-off (reinvest at 15–20% incremental ROIC rather than repurchase at a market multiple), but it does leave per-share value creation to organic earnings growth rather than share count reduction.

---

## 4. Dividends & Organic Reinvestment

| Metric | FY2021 | FY2022 | FY2023 | FY2024 | FY2025 | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|
| Payout ratio | 0% | 0% | 0% | 0% | 0% | FY2025 10-K, Item 5, p.19 |
| Dividend coverage (FCF) | N/A | N/A | N/A | N/A | N/A | No dividend paid |
| FCF strict (CFO minus gross capex) | ($14,726)M | ($16,893)M | $32,217M | $32,878M | $7,695M | Capital IQ Cash Flow; FY2025 10-K, p.36 |
| Company-disclosed FCF | N/A | N/A | N/A | $38,219M | $11,194M | FY2025 10-K, p.28 (FCF reconciliation) |
| Gross capex / CFO | 131.8% | 136.1% | 62.1% | 71.6% | 94.5% | Computed: capex ÷ CFO by year |
| ROIC (NOPAT/avg invested capital, ~10% tax) | 17.2% | 6.9% | 15.1% | 20.1% | 18.1% | Computed (see below) |
| Incremental ROIC (ΔNOPAT/ΔIC) | N/A | (37.3%) | 34.6% | 24.8% | 15.9% | Computed (see below) |
| SBC as % of CFO | 27.5% | 41.9% | 28.3% | 19.0% | 13.9% | Capital IQ Supplemental; corroborated FY2025 10-K |

**ROIC computation methodology:**
- NOPAT = EBIT × (1 − 10%), using a conservative flat 10% tax rate (effective tax rates vary: ~7% FY2025, ~9% FY2024). The 10% rate is used as a through-cycle normalisation.
- Invested capital (IC) = total common equity + total financial debt (short-term borrowings + current LT debt + LT debt) − total cash and short-term investments. Lease obligations are excluded from financial debt (they are tracked separately via the broad net-debt basis).
- Beginning-of-year IC uses prior FY year-end balance sheet. FY2020 IC = $99,870M (equity $138,245M + fin. debt $57,674M − cash $96,049M).
- [Capital IQ Balance Sheet; FY2025 10-K, p.39 (Balance Sheet); FY2021 10-K Balance Sheet]

**Incremental ROIC detail:**

| Period | ΔNOPAT | ΔInvested Capital | Incremental ROIC |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| FY2021→FY2022 | ($10,378)M | +$27,789M | (37.3%) |
| FY2022→FY2023 | +$21,154M | +$61,050M | 34.6% |
| FY2023→FY2024 | +$28,567M | +$115,074M | 24.8% |
| FY2024→FY2025 | +$10,244M | +$64,378M | 15.9% |

**Interpretation:**
- FY2022 incremental ROIC was negative because Amazon significantly over-built fulfillment capacity during COVID and absorbed large equity investment losses from Rivian. This was a recognized misallocation year (EBIT fell from $24.9B to $13.3B on a $27.8B increase in invested capital).
- FY2023–FY2024 incremental ROIC of 35% and 25% respectively represents strong compounding evidence: each incremental dollar of capital deployed in those years generated well above the 9–10% estimated cost of capital.
- FY2025 incremental ROIC of 15.9% remains above cost of capital, but signals the initial wave of AWS/AI capex is compressing returns per-dollar-deployed. AWS segment capex jumped from $53.3B (FY2024) to $96.5B (FY2025), and the full revenue benefit of that spend is a 12–24 month lag.
- The FY2025 decline from 24.8% to 15.9% incremental ROIC is expected and not alarming — it reflects the front-loading of capex ahead of confirmed AWS backlog ($364B as of Q1 2026 per Q1 2026 Earnings Call, Apr 29, 2026). However, if FY2026–2027 incremental ROIC falls below 12%, the AWS investment thesis weakens materially.

**Balance-sheet prudence:**
- Net debt / EBITDA (broad basis, including leases): declining from 0.87x (FY2023) to 0.38x (FY2025). Strict net cash position at FY2025: ~$46.1B. [FY2025 10-K, p.39; Capital IQ Ratios]
- LTM Mar-31-2026 net debt (broad basis) rose to $92.5B, driven by $15.7B in new long-term debt issued November 2025 and further Q1 2026 debt issuance ($54B+ increment per Capital IQ). Strict basis at Mar-31-2026 is approximately net cash ~$12.6B (Q1 2026 10-Q balance sheet).
- Total contractual commitments: $439.7B as of Dec 31, 2025 — a very large off-balance-sheet obligation set, but all disclosed and asset-backed. [FY2025 10-K, Note 7, p.59]
- EBIT/interest coverage: 35.2x (FY2025). No solvency risk. [Capital IQ Ratios]

---

## 5. Capital-Allocation Read

Amazon's capital allocation over FY2021–FY2025 is **value-creative but with a clear flaw**: organic reinvestment in AWS and AI infrastructure is the dominant use (90.5% of CFO) and is generating returns (18–20% ROIC in FY2024–FY2025) that comfortably exceed the cost of capital; however, persistent SBC dilution (+5.0% net share count over five years) with a dormant buyback program means per-share value is created entirely through earnings power growth, not capital return. The best use of capital is unambiguously organic reinvestment — incremental ROIC of 25–35% in FY2023–FY2024 is exceptional by any standard. The worst use is the share-dilution-without-offsetting-buybacks dynamic: management sits on a $6.1B authorization while issuing equity-equivalent compensation at scale. M&A (MGM, One Medical) is bolt-on, rationally priced, and not the story — these are small deals relative to Amazon's scale. The single biggest capital-allocation signal is the $131.8B capex in FY2025 (a 3.1x capex-to-D&A ratio) guided to approximately $200B in FY2026: this is the largest organic capital bet in corporate history by dollar quantum, and the degree to which AWS backlog converts to revenue over the next 12–24 months will determine whether the incremental ROIC holds at 15%+ or collapses below cost of capital.

**Verdict: value-creative / disciplined**, with one ongoing blemish (share dilution not offset by buybacks).

## management-governance / 03_incentives-and-compensation.md

_Source: `03_incentives-and-compensation.md`_

# Incentives & Compensation — AMZN

**Reporting currency:** USD. **Fiscal year end:** December 31. **As of:** 2026-07-04.
**Jurisdiction:** US SEC (10-K, DEF 14A). **Partial-data cap applied:** Incentive alignment max 50/100 — the 2026 DEF 14A proxy statement (filed April 2026) is not in the data pool. Compensation structure and aggregate SBC are partially reconstructed from the FY2025 10-K, Capital IQ Takeover Defenses/Public Company Profile, and web-sourced information (labelled throughout).

---

## 1. Compensation Structure

| Executive | Base | Annual Bonus | Long-Term Incentive | Cash vs Equity Mix | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---|---|---|
| CEO — Andrew R. (Andy) Jassy | $365,000 | $0 (none — no annual cash bonus for any NEO except new hires) | Time-vested RSUs from a 10-year grant made at CEO appointment (July 2021); 61,000 shares total granted, ~$212M fair value at grant; no new grant since 2021; vesting: 5%/15%/40%/40% in years 1–4, back-loaded; FY2025 SEC-disclosed total comp $2.07M (FY2024: $40.1M driven by RSU vesting; FY2025 $43M of unvested RSUs remain per 2026 proxy) | ~99.9% equity (of lifetime grant value); FY2025 "reported" total: $365K base + $0 new equity + $1.7M security/travel | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 (10b5-1 plan); Capital IQ Takeover Defenses (compensation philosophy); Web: 2026 DEF 14A filed Apr 8, 2026, as cited in Yahoo Finance Apr 2026 and salary.com (web-sourced, unverified) |
| CFO — Brian T. Olsavsky | $365,000 | $0 (same structure — no annual bonus) | RSU grants at intervals (not annually); most recent reported: 106,374 RSUs granted in 2026, vesting 2027–2032; FY2024 total comp ~$25.7M (RSU vesting year); FY2025 reported comp $372K (inter-grant year, no RSU vesting) | ~99% equity (in RSU vesting years); ~100% cash in inter-grant years | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 (10b5-1 plan termination Nov 2025); Web: StockTitan Form 4 filing (106,374 RSUs) and salary.com (web-sourced, unverified) |

**Notes on the table:**
- "Reported" total compensation in the 2026 proxy Summary Compensation Table follows SEC methodology: it reports the grant-date fair value of any new equity grants in the year. Because no new RSU grant was made to Jassy in 2025 (or since 2021), his 2025 table total is $2.07M — almost entirely security and travel costs. The economic value of RSUs vesting from the 2021 grant (FY2025: ~$43M vested) does not appear as a new grant in the 2025 table.
- The FY2025 total SBC expense charged to the income statement was $24.0B (FY2024: $22.0B; FY2023: $19.5B) — this is company-wide for ~1.6 million employees. The CEO's and CFO's individual RSU values are a negligible fraction of total SBC. [FY2025 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.36]
- Jeff Bezos (Executive Chair) received $81,840 in base salary with no equity grants reported in FY2025. His economic stake is through his ~8.8% ownership (~950M shares) which is not a compensation item. [Web: 2026 DEF 14A, web-sourced, unverified]

---

## 2. Performance Metrics

| Plan | Metric | Weight | Per-Share/Returns or Size-Based? | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|
| Annual bonus | **None** — no annual cash incentive plan for named executive officers except at new hire | N/A | N/A — no annual bonus plan exists | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B; Capital IQ Takeover Defenses: "no executive severance agreements; no annual cash incentives"; Web: 2026 DEF 14A (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Long-term incentive (RSU) — primary vehicle | **Time-vested only** — RSUs vest on a fixed schedule regardless of financial performance; the only implicit metric is stock price (shares vest into common equity; realized value = shares × stock price at vesting) | 100% | **Per-share (implicit, via stock price)** — no ROIC, no EPS, no operating income, no revenue, no TSR hurdle required; vesting is purely time-based | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses (compensation section): "compensation tied to long-term performance reflected primarily in the stock price"; FY2025 10-K, Note 1 (stock-based compensation accounting policy, p.44): "RSUs vest based on service period"; Web: 2026 DEF 14A philosophy statement; ggsitc.com executive compensation teardown (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Long-term incentive (RSU) — vesting schedule | Back-loaded time schedule: 5% year 1, 15% year 2, 40% year 3, 40% year 4 (CEO 2021 grant: 10-year schedule, >80% vesting in final 5 years of the grant, through 2031) | N/A | Per-share (time, no performance hurdle) | Web: Amazon RSU guides citing proxy disclosures (web-sourced, unverified); FY2025 10-K, p.1596–1599 (equity compensation policy) |

**Classification of each implicit metric:**

| Metric (implicit) | Explicit or Implicit? | Per-Share/Returns or Size-Based? | Incentive Effect |
|---|---|---|---|
| Stock price appreciation | Implicit (no explicit target set) | Per-share | Executives benefit only if stock price rises; wealth tracks total shareholder return over a multi-year period |
| Operating income / ROIC | Not a metric | — | Not explicitly rewarded; no target or hurdle in the plan |
| Revenue / AWS growth | Not a metric | — | Not explicitly rewarded |
| EPS (diluted) | Not a metric | — | Not set as a threshold |
| Absolute EBITDA | Not a metric | — | Not set as a threshold |

**Conclusion on metrics:** Amazon uses exclusively time-vested RSUs with no explicit performance hurdles of any kind. No annual bonus plan. No LTIP with ROIC, EPS, or TSR hurdles. The link to per-share value is entirely through stock price — but it is implicit and unconditional: executives receive shares regardless of operating performance, as long as they remain employed. This is a structurally unusual model among S&P 500 mega-caps. Its effect is directional: Jassy cannot earn a cash bonus by growing revenue but hurting margins; his wealth accumulates only if the stock price rises. However, it also means mediocre but stable performance would still vest shares, since there is no performance condition.

In FY2024, 211,000 shares vested with a realized value of ~$38.5M (at AMZN ~$182/share at vesting); FY2025 economic comp estimated ~$43M based on next tranche of vesting at FY2025 AMZN price range. The SEC-reported headline figure of $2.07M is misleading for year-over-year benchmarking purposes. | Web: GeekWire Apr 2025, Amazon 2026 proxy (web-sourced, unverified); FY2025 10-K, Item 9B (vesting plan details) |

Jassy's unvested RSUs have appreciated in line with shareholders. The compensation structure does reward per-share gains: a flat or declining stock would leave unvested RSUs worth less. However, no performance condition prevents vesting even if performance is poor — the link is indirect. | Capital IQ Public Company Profile: AMZN $239.59 (Jul 1, 2026); Web: 2021 CEO appointment stock price (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Say-on-pay support (2025 Annual Meeting, re FY2024 comp) | **78%** for / **22%** against. This follows a multi-year declining opposition pattern: 2022 meeting: 56% for (44% against); 2023 meeting: 68.5% for (31.5% against); 2024 meeting: 77.8% for (22.2% against); 2025 meeting: 78% for. The 22% opposition, while declining, reflects persistent institutional concern about the lack of performance hurdles in RSU grants. | Web: Morningstar, SOC Investment Group vote-no letter Apr 2025 (web-sourced, unverified); Amazon 2026 DEF 14A (reports prior year vote) (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Say-on-pay support (2026 Annual Meeting, re FY2025 comp, held May 20, 2026) | **~94%** for / ~6% against (7,391,737,243 for vs 470,466,853 against). Substantial improvement from prior years. | Web: Amazon 2026 8-K (Form 8-K re: shareholder meeting, filed post-May 20, 2026), as reported in web sources (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Votes-against trend | Opposition fell from 44% (2022) to 22% (2024) to 6% (2026) — a clear, sustained reduction in institutional opposition. The improvement coincides with the operating income recovery that generated strong stock price appreciation, validating the time-vested RSU model in hindsight. | Web: Multiple sources (web-sourced, unverified) |

---

## 4. Incentive Alignment Read

Amazon's compensation model is the simplest in the S&P 500 mega-cap peer group: no annual bonus plan, no explicit performance metrics of any kind, and a single vehicle — time-vested restricted stock units — that link executive wealth entirely to stock price appreciation over a multi-year horizon. The stated philosophy (per the 2026 DEF 14A) is that this "directly aligns executives' compensation with the returns we deliver to shareholders." That claim has economic substance: Jassy cannot enrich himself by growing revenue while destroying margins; his financial outcome is bound to the equity market's verdict on Amazon's long-term per-share value. The biggest limitation of this model is that vesting is unconditional — there is no performance hurdle (no ROIC minimum, no TSR threshold, no EPS floor) that would prevent RSU vesting even if operating results deteriorated. A management team that ran the company at mediocre-but-stable results could still vest their full grant. In practice, the model has worked during the Jassy era because operating income grew 6.5x (FY2022–FY2025) and the stock re-rated, but this is not a structural protection — it is a favorable outcome that happened to coincide with the time-vested schedule.

The single biggest incentive signal: **management is paid in equity that they cannot convert to cash except by holding for years (the back-loaded vesting schedule), which removes the short-termism of quarterly earnings management**. It does not, however, create an explicit incentive to deliver returns above the cost of capital (ROIC) or to improve per-share metrics beyond the stock price.

**Verdict: Mixed.** The structure is directionally per-share-oriented (equity only, no revenue or EBITDA bonuses), but lacks explicit returns-based or per-share performance hurdles. The absence of an annual bonus or any size-based metric is a structural positive; the absence of any performance condition on RSU vesting is a structural limitation.

## management-governance / 04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md
