# Ownership & Insider Behavior — AMZN

**Reporting standard:** US GAAP. **Currency:** USD. **Fiscal year end:** December 31. **As of:** 2026-07-04.
**Jurisdiction:** US SEC (10-K, DEF 14A, Form 4, Schedule 13D/13G). **Partial-data cap applied:** Shareholder friendliness max 60 — no complete beneficial ownership table from the 2026 DEF 14A proxy is in the data pool; no comprehensive Form 4 transaction log or Capital IQ insider-transaction export is in the pool. Named insiders and float are available from the Capital IQ Public Company Profile (Jul 1, 2026) and FY2025 10-K Item 9B; web-sourced ownership/transaction data is labeled as such throughout.

---

## 1. Ownership

| Holder | % Held | Share Class | Bought or Granted? | Source |
|---|---:|---|---|---|
| Jeffrey P. Bezos (Founder & Executive Chair) | ~8.8% (~882–950M shares) | Common stock (1 vote/share) | **Bought** — Bezos's entire stake originates from founding Amazon in 1994 and long-term retention; he has not received new RSU grants in his Executive Chair role; stake is being reduced through orderly open-market sales and charitable gifts | Web: 2026 DEF 14A beneficial ownership table as reported by multiple outlets (web-sourced, unverified); FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 (10b5-1 plan Nov 14, 2025) |
| Andrew R. Jassy (President & CEO) | <0.1% | Common stock (1 vote/share) | **Granted** — Jassy's equity comes from a back-loaded 10-year RSU grant made at his CEO appointment in July 2021 (61,000 shares total, ~$212M grant-date fair value); no open-market purchases | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 (10b5-1 plan Nov 14, 2025); agent 03 incentives cross-module; web-sourced, unverified for share count |
| Other insiders (aggregate — named officers + directors ex-Bezos) | <0.2% (estimated) | Common stock (1 vote/share) | **Granted** — all other executive officers hold RSUs granted as compensation; no disclosed open-market purchases by any named officer other than Bezos; board directors hold equity from director grants | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74; Capital IQ Public Company Profile, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Vanguard Group (institutional) | ~6.8% | Common stock | N/A — passive index ownership | Web: 2026 proxy / 13F filings as reported (web-sourced, unverified); Capital IQ Public Company Profile (Vanguard Capital Management listed as investor, Jul 1, 2026) |
| BlackRock (institutional) | ~6.8% | Common stock | N/A — passive index ownership | Web: 2026 proxy / 13F filings as reported (web-sourced, unverified) |
| State Street Corporation (institutional) | ~3.6% | Common stock | N/A — passive index ownership | Web: 13F filings as reported (web-sourced, unverified); Capital IQ Public Company Profile (State Street Corporation listed as investor, Jul 1, 2026) |
| Float (public) | 90.9% | Common stock (1 vote/share) | N/A | Capital IQ Public Company Profile, Jul 1, 2026 (delayed quote last updated Jul 1, 2026 10:28 AM) |

**Total named insider ownership %:** Bezos ~8.8%; all other named insiders (Jassy + other officers + board) combined estimated <0.5%. Total insider ownership approximately **9–9.5%** (web-sourced, unverified; the proxy beneficial ownership table is not in the data pool).

**Single-class structure.** Amazon has one class of common stock with one vote per share. The Capital IQ Takeover Defenses report (Jul 1, 2026) confirms: "Dual Class Stock: No"; "Common Stock Voting Rights: 1." Bezos's influence derives from economic stake size, not from any structural super-voting mechanism. [Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026]

**Shares outstanding.** 10,757.1M shares as of Jul 1, 2026 (Capital IQ Public Company Profile). 10,757.1M × $239.59 = $2,576B market cap. Shares outstanding increased from 10,383M (Dec 2023) to 10,597M (Dec 2024) to 10,734M (Dec 2025) to 10,757M (Q1 2026) — a net increase of ~374M shares (3.6%) over two years driven by RSU vesting net of Bezos sales. [Capital IQ Historical Capitalization tab, FY2024–Q1 2026; FY2025 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Stockholders' Equity, p.40]

---

## 2. Insider Transactions (last 12 months)

**Pool data (FY2025 10-K Item 9B — November 2025 disclosures):**

| Insider | Buy / Sell | Amount (max shares, planned) | Date | Notes (10b5-1?) | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|---|
| Jeffrey P. Bezos (Founder & Executive Chair) | Sell (planned) | Up to 15,000,000 shares through Feb 26, 2027 | Plan adopted Nov 14, 2025 | Rule 10b5-1(c) plan — pre-scheduled orderly sale, not distress selling | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 |
| Andrew R. Jassy (President & CEO) | Sell (planned) | Up to 142,224 shares through Dec 31, 2026 | Plan adopted Nov 14, 2025 | Rule 10b5-1(c) plan — RSU diversification sales, small vs total shares outstanding | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 |
| David A. Zapolsky (SVP, Chief Global Affairs & Legal Officer) | Sell (planned) | Up to 64,059 shares through Nov 30, 2026 | Plan adopted Nov 3, 2025 | Rule 10b5-1(c) plan | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 |
| Douglas J. Herrington (CEO Worldwide Amazon Stores) | Sell (planned) | Up to 132,937 shares through Dec 31, 2026 | Plan adopted Nov 10, 2025 | Rule 10b5-1(c) plan | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 |
| Keith B. Alexander (Director, not standing for re-election) | Sell (planned) | Up to 1,800 shares through Nov 4, 2026 | Plan adopted Nov 11, 2025 | Rule 10b5-1(c) plan; immaterial amount | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 |
| Shelley L. Reynolds (VP, Worldwide Controller) | Sell (planned) | Up to 9,744 shares through Nov 29, 2026 | Plan adopted Nov 11, 2025 | Rule 10b5-1(c) plan | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 |
| Brian T. Olsavsky (CFO) | Plan terminated (no shares sold under prior plan) | Prior plan: up to 53,249 shares | Terminated Nov 19, 2025 | Plan originally adopted May 20, 2025; terminated before execution period closed; no shares sold | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 |
| All named insiders (Q1 2026) | No new plans adopted | None | Jan–Mar 2026 | Q1 2026 10-Q Item 5 (Trading Arrangements): "None" — no new plans in Q1 2026 | Q1 2026 10-Q, Item 5, p.48 |

**Web-sourced context (labeled unverified):** Bezos conducted a ~$5 billion concentrated selling spree in July 2025 (approximately 25 million shares at ~$221–$233/share), executed under a pre-set 10b5-1 plan. From late 2025 onward, his Form 4 filings shifted primarily to charitable gift transactions (code "G") rather than open-market sales. As of June/July 2026, Bezos holds approximately 882–950 million shares (~8.8% of outstanding). [Web: 13finsight.com citing Form 4 filings; web-sourced, unverified; note: these figures may not be filed with EDGAR in the pool but are consistent with the 10-K's 10b5-1 plan disclosure.]

**Net insider activity: Net selling.** All disclosed plans involve sales; no named insider has filed an open-market purchase in the pool period. Bezos's plan covers up to 15 million shares — roughly 1.6% of his current stake — which is a gradual diversification rather than a wholesale exit. All sales are under pre-set 10b5-1(c) plans that were adopted in November 2025; all sales are pre-scheduled. The CFO (Olsavsky) terminated his plan before shares were sold, which removes the most CFO-departure-signal-sensitive selling from the table.

**No open-market buys.** No named insider or director has made an open-market purchase of Amazon shares in the disclosed 12-month period. This is consistent with Amazon's compensation model (RSUs, no stock-option grants requiring purchase) and does not represent an unusual pattern for a large-cap S&P 500 company. Amazon's executive team accumulated their shares primarily through career service (Bezos) or grant vesting (Jassy and other officers), not open-market conviction purchases.

**Distinction — bought vs granted:**
- Bezos: **Bought** (founder equity, held since 1994; not a grant).
- All other named insiders: **Granted** (RSUs from compensation packages; no documented open-market purchases by any current officer other than Bezos).

---

## 3. Pledging & Encumbrance

| Insider | Shares Pledged | % of Their Holding | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Jeffrey P. Bezos | 0 (no pledge disclosed) | 0% | FY2025 10-K (no insider pledge disclosure; the 10-K's only pledge reference is corporate-level: cash/securities pledged as collateral for real estate and third-party seller obligations — Note 3, p.54 and footnote to Note 5 on cash/securities); Capital IQ Takeover Defenses: no insider pledging provision triggered |
| All other named insiders | 0 (none disclosed) | 0% | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B and Notes to Financial Statements; disqualifier scan |

**No pledging disclosed.** The FY2025 10-K contains no disclosure of any executive officer or director pledging Amazon shares as collateral for personal loans. The note (FY2025 10-K, Note 3, p.54) that references "pledged assets" relates to Amazon the company pledging cash and real estate as collateral for letters of credit and tenant improvement leases — not insider share pledging. US proxy statements typically contain an anti-pledging / anti-hedging policy in the Compensation section; the 2026 DEF 14A (absent from the pool) would contain the formal policy. However, no material insider pledging is disclosed or evidenced in any pool filing.

**Cross-reference to `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan`:** Disqualifier #2 (>50% promoter/insider shares pledged) — Not triggered. The disqualifier scan explicitly states: "No controlling promoter / founding-family pledge structure exists. The 10-K... discloses no material insider pledging." [business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md, Disqualifier #2]

---

## 4. Control Structure

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Dual-class / super-voting | **None.** Amazon has a single class of common stock with one vote per share. "Dual Class Stock: No"; "Common Stock Voting Rights: 1"; "Time-Phased Unequal Voting Rights: No." | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Controlled-company status | **No.** Amazon does not qualify as a "controlled company" under Nasdaq listing rules — no single holder owns >50% of voting power. Bezos holds ~8.8%; even combined with the other named insiders (<0.5%), total insider voting power is well below 50%. | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026; Capital IQ Public Company Profile (float 90.9%), Jul 1, 2026 |
| Shareholder bloc with board rights | **None.** No shareholder agreement, voting trust, or side agreement grants any holder board nomination or veto rights. Bezos's influence is through economic stake size only. The Nominating & Corporate Governance Committee nominates directors; shareholders elect them by majority vote (not plurality). | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026; 2026 DEF 14A (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Classified / staggered board | **None.** All 11 nominees stand for annual election. "Classified Board: No." | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Poison pill | **None.** "Active Poison Pill: None Reported." | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Bezos chair/CEO separation | CEO and Chair roles are separated: Jassy (CEO) and Bezos (Executive Chair). A Lead Independent Director (Jamie Gorelick) presides over executive sessions. | FY2025 10-K, p.4–5; 2026 DEF 14A (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Controlling-owner objective alignment (Filter 6) | **Not a Filter 6 concern.** Bezos is a founder who built Amazon over three decades, holds his equity primarily for long-term economic appreciation, and has no structural mechanism to extract value at the expense of minority shareholders (no dual class, no special dividend to himself, no related-party transactions). The company operates with no government ownership, is not a subsidiary of a parent, and is not a sprawling unrelated conglomerate (its segments — retail, cloud, advertising — are operationally integrated). Filter 6 (RF-OWN-004) does not apply. | CLAUDE.md §24 Filter 6; Module_Rules.md Score Cap Rules |

**Unaligned-owner test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 6):**
(a) **Government control:** Not applicable — Amazon is a private corporation, no government ownership.
(b) **Listed subsidiary of a parent:** Not applicable — Amazon is the parent, not a subsidiary.
(c) **Sprawling unrelated-diversified conglomerate:** Not applicable — Amazon's three segments (North America retail, International retail, AWS) are operationally integrated around the same customer, technology, and logistics base; advertising serves retail; AWS serves enterprise. This is a multi-product platform, not an unrelated conglomerate.

No RF-OWN-004 flag applies. No shareholder-friendliness cap from Filter 6. The controlling-owner's objective (Bezos as the largest individual holder) is long-term per-share value appreciation; his incentives are aligned with minority holders, not opposed to them.

## 4A. Market Conduct

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Insider buys/sells vs subsequent results (timing) | Bezos conducted a ~$5 billion selling spree in July 2025 (~25M shares at $221–$233). Amazon stock subsequently rose to $278 by late 2025 before retreating. The selling occurred before Q2 2025 results (which beat consensus by $5.6B revenue and $0.36 EPS). The selling was executed under a pre-set 10b5-1 plan adopted before the quarter closed — this is the structural protection that pre-set plans provide against insider-timing allegations. Amazon beat consensus in all four quarters covering this period. The selling preceded strong results, not weak ones, which is the opposite of the adverse timing pattern. | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74 (10b5-1 plan disclosures); Web: Form 4 filings via 13finsight.com (web-sourced, unverified); Capital IQ Estimates Surprise tab (beat data) |
| Unusual price / volume before announcements | No exchange clarification request, no SEC inquiry, and no unusual volume event is disclosed in any filing in the pool. Amazon's earnings releases follow the regular quarterly reporting calendar. | FY2025 10-K; Q1 2026 10-Q; Capital IQ Public Company Profile (no regulatory action cited) |
| Exchange clarification sought for a price move | None disclosed or evidenced. Amazon is a US NASDAQ-listed company; NASDAQ does not typically issue query letters for price moves at large-caps. No unusual regulatory exchange enquiry is evidenced in the pool. | FY2025 10-K; pool extracts |
| Institutional (institutional holder) holding trend; any sharp high-quality-holder exit | Vanguard (~6.8%) and BlackRock (~6.8%) are passive index holders — their holdings move with index weightings, not conviction changes. State Street (~3.6%) similarly. No sharp exit by a high-conviction active manager is evidenced in the pool; the Capital IQ Public Company Profile (Jul 1, 2026) does not flag any significant institutional holder departure. The Vanguard 13G amendment (Vanguard Group filing an exit followed by Vanguard Capital Management LLC filing a new 13G) appears to be a technical entity restructuring, not an economic reduction in Vanguard's position. | Capital IQ Public Company Profile, Jul 1, 2026 (lists State Street, T. Rowe Price as ongoing investors); Web: 13F data (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Trade-timing red flag | **No RF-MKT-001 or RF-MKT-002 triggered.** All insider selling was under pre-set 10b5-1 plans. Bezos's July 2025 selling preceded strong (not weak) results. No evidence of insider selling before weak results. | FY2025 10-K, Item 9B, p.74; Capital IQ Surprise tab |

---

## 5. Ownership / Insider Read

Bezos owns roughly 8.8% of Amazon, and that stake originates from founding the company — not from grants or option exercises. This is the most meaningful single fact: the largest individual shareholder built what he owns over three decades. All other named insiders (Jassy, Olsavsky, and the rest of the executive team) hold RSU grants with no evidence of open-market purchases, meaning their alignment is real but shallower and grant-dependent, not conviction-driven cash investment.

The net insider signal is selling, but it is entirely pre-scheduled under Rule 10b5-1(c) plans — the structural equivalent of saying "I will sell on a pre-set schedule regardless of what I know." Bezos's selling preceded strong quarterly results, not weak ones, and the CFO's plan was terminated before any sales. There is no adverse timing signal.

The biggest control point for minority holders is the absence of a dual-class structure. Bezos's ~8.8% stake gives him no special voting leverage beyond his economic size; the board is elected annually by majority vote; no poison pill or staggered board exists; and the Lead Independent Director is structurally independent of him. Amazon is one of the rare founder-chaired mega-caps where the founder's influence is purely proportional to his economic stake — a positive structural feature for minority holders.

# Board & Shareholder Rights — AMZN

**Reporting standard:** US GAAP. **Jurisdiction:** US / SEC. **Fiscal year end:** December 31. **As of:** 2026-07-04.

**Note:** Amazon has no controlling promoter / family holding company structure. Item 13 (Certain Relationships and Related Transactions) is incorporated by reference to the 2026 proxy. The proxy (web-sourced) confirms no material related-party transaction other than the routine, immaterial ordinary-course payments to Weeks-affiliated (Corning) and Ng-affiliated (DeepLearning.AI) entities — both reviewed and cleared by the Board as not impairing independence. No RPT note appears in the FY2025 10-K financial statements, and the disqualifier scan confirmed no RPT above 25% of revenue or expenses. The core leakage question is answered: no value is leaking to a promoter or related group. The economic interests of the Board and management are aligned with public shareholders, not with a separate controlling entity.

**Cross-reference to `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan`:** Disqualifier #3 (RPTs >25% of revenue/expenses) — Not triggered. No material RPTs disclosed.

## 4. Takeover Defenses & Voting Rights

| Feature | Present? | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Poison pill | No | "Active Poison Pill: None Reported" — Amazon has no shareholder rights plan in effect | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses report, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Classified / staggered board | No | "Classified Board: No"; all directors are up for election annually; board term length = 1 year per director | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Dual-class / unequal voting | No | "Dual Class Stock: No"; "Common Stock Voting Rights: 1" (one vote per share); "Time-Phased Unequal Voting Rights: No" | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026; 2026 DEF 14A (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Director voting standard | Majority (not plurality) | "Directors are elected by a majority of the votes cast" — majority standard gives shareholders real ability to withhold directors | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Shareholders' ability to call meetings | Yes, at 25% threshold | Special meetings may be called by holders of not less than 25% of all votes entitled to be cast on the issue proposed; also callable by the Chair, CEO, President, or the Board | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 (Bylaws language verbatim) |
| Shareholders' ability to act by written consent | Yes, at 50% threshold | "Shareholders Ability to Act By Written Consent (without Board Approval): Yes"; requires 50% approval — meaningful but not a supermajority | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Supermajority for charter/bylaw amendment | No | "Supermajority Shareholder Approval Required to Amend Charter: No"; "Supermajority Required to Amend Bylaws: No"; only 50% needed — shareholders retain amending power | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Board authority to change board size | Yes (Board only) | "Board Can Change Board Size: Yes"; "Shareholders Can Change Board Size: No" — the Board alone controls its own size between annual meetings | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Blank check preferred stock | Yes | "Blank Check Preferred Stock: Yes" — the Board may issue preferred shares with rights determined by the Board; this is a standard Delaware corporation feature and a potential takeover defense mechanism | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Proxy access | Yes | The 2026 proxy states shareholders have a "proxy access right on market-standard terms" (web-sourced, unverified) | 2026 DEF 14A (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Advance notice for nominations | 90 days | Days of Advance Notice Required for Director Nominations: 90 | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Anti-greenmail | Not reported | No anti-greenmail provision noted in Capital IQ data | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Cumulative voting | No | "Cumulative Voting for Board Seats: No" — one share, one vote per director seat | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Removal of directors | For cause only | "Removal of Directors Only for Cause: No" — actually, the Capital IQ field reads "Board Can Change Board Size: Yes" and removal is not limited to cause only per the available data | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| CEO succession plan | Yes | "Company Has CEO Succession Plan: Yes" | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Lead/Presiding Director | Yes | "Board of Directors Lead / Presiding Director Provision: Yes" | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Board performance reviews | Yes | "Board Performance Reviews: Yes" | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| Executive sessions of non-executive directors | Yes | "The independent directors of the Board shall meet periodically in executive session" (Bylaws verbatim) | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |
| State of incorporation | Delaware | Standard US large-cap jurisdiction | Capital IQ Takeover Defenses, Jul 1, 2026 |

**Overall takeover-defense assessment:** Amazon's charter and bylaw structure is notably shareholder-friendly for a large-cap US company. No poison pill. No staggered board. No dual-class shares. Majority (not plurality) director voting. No supermajority amendment requirements. Proxy access at market terms. Shareholders can call meetings at 25%. The only defensive features are blank-check preferred stock (a standard Delaware provision), a 90-day advance notice window (standard), and the Board's unilateral authority to resize its own board — all of which are conventional. The absence of dual-class stock is a key positive: Bezos' influence flows from his 8.8% economic stake, not from superior voting rights.

## 4A. Shareholder Voting & Dilution

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Say-on-pay vote trend | 2022: ~56.1% for (43.9% against); 2023: ~68.5% for (31.5% against); 2024: ~77.8% for (22.2% against); 2025: 78% for (22% against). The opposition peaked at 43.9% in 2022 and has partially declined but remained persistently at 22% in both 2024 and 2025. 22% against is above the 20% materiality threshold. | Web: Morningstar (web-sourced, unverified); 2026 DEF 14A (web-sourced, unverified, citing 78% at "2025 Annual Meeting") |
| Proxy-advisor recommendations (ISS / Glass Lewis) | ISS recommended "against" Amazon's say-on-pay resolution in each of the past three years (web-sourced, unverified). Glass Lewis joined the NYC Comptroller and state pension funds in opposing the re-election of a specific director in 2022. No 2025 specific ISS/Glass Lewis recommendation confirmed from pool sources. | Web: SOC Investment Group letter (web-sourced, unverified); NYC Comptroller press release (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Institutional opposition to any resolution | 2022: NYC Comptroller and allied state pension funds, joined by Glass Lewis, opposed the re-election of Judith McGrath (then chair of the Compensation Committee) on worker safety grounds; ~27% of outside shares voted against her. McGrath is no longer on the board (left after that meeting). Board took the signal seriously: compensation practices evolved, vote improved from 56% to 78% over three years. | Web: NYC Comptroller press release (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Shareholder proposals at 2025 Annual Meeting | Proposals requesting mandatory CEO/Chair separation, advertising risk reporting, alternative emissions reporting, AI oversight Board structure, packaging materials, warehouse conditions, and data/AI usage oversight — all voted down with the Board's "against" recommendation. None passed. | Web: Governance Intelligence (web-sourced, unverified); 2025 proxy (web-sourced, unverified) |
| Dilution history | Shares outstanding grew from 10,383M (Dec 2023) to 10,731M (Dec 2025) — a net increase of 348M shares (+3.4%) over two years, entirely from RSU vesting; $6.1B of the $10B buyback authorization from March 2022 remains unused as of Dec 2025. The buyback program is dormant while the company reinvests heavily in AI capex. | FY2025 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Stockholders' Equity, p.40; Note 8, p.62 |

**Say-on-pay flag:** At 22% against in 2024 and 2025, Amazon's say-on-pay opposition exceeds the 20% red-flag threshold in the Red-Flag Trigger Engine. The trend has improved materially (from 44% in 2022) but has plateaued at 22%. This is an ongoing governance friction point, though it does not implicate fraud, self-dealing, or entrenchment. It reflects continued disagreement between institutional shareholders / proxy advisors and the Board on pay quantum and structure.

---

## 5. Minority-Shareholder Protection Read

Amazon's board is genuinely independent in structure: 83% independent directors, all major committees fully independent and chaired by independents, a Lead Independent Director (Gorelick) who engages directly with shareholders, majority voting for directors, and no defensive measures that suppress shareholder voice. The chair/CEO split (Bezos/Jassy) is real — Bezos' influence is economic (8.8% ownership), not structural (he has one vote per share, same as every other shareholder). There is no dual-class shield, no classified board, no supermajority protection, and no poison pill. Rights — special meetings at 25%, written consent at 50%, proxy access, no supermajority barrier — are better than typical for a company of this size and complexity.

The single biggest risk to minority holders is not structural but behavioral: the Board's compensation committee has failed to bring say-on-pay support above 80% despite three years of effort, and ISS has recommended against management's pay proposals in three consecutive years. This is a governance friction point that cannot be dismissed, even as it has materially improved from the 2022 nadir. The long tenures of Stonesifer (29 years) and Rubinstein (15 years) raise standard questions about director freshness, though no evidence of captured independence exists.

Additionally, Wendell Weeks — Audit Committee member — is a sitting CEO and Chair of Corning Incorporated; his dual commitment is a mild structural concern. Brad Smith's recent departure from Humana (April 2025) reduced one overboarding concern.

There is no §24 Filter 6 (Structurally Unaligned Controlling Owner) concern here. Bezos' ownership aligns his interests with per-share value; he does not control a separate parent entity extracting value from Amazon, nor is Amazon a subsidiary of a value-maximizing third party. The RF-OWN-004 filter does not trip.

**Verdict: Adequate.** The board is structurally independent, rights are intact and genuinely shareholder-friendly, and no entrenchment mechanism is in place. The persistent ~22% say-on-pay opposition and the long tenure of two directors keep the rating from "strong protection."

## Board & Shareholder Rights Score

| Component | Score | Max Score | Evidence |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Board independence | 18 | 20 | 83% independent, all committees independent, Lead Independent Director in place; minor deduction for Board's sole authority to resize board between meetings |
| True independence / tenure / overboarding | 14 | 20 | Stonesifer's 29-year tenure (-3) and Weeks' CEO-of-another-company dual role (-2) are genuine concerns; balanced by meaningful recent refreshment (Ng, Smith) and no evidence of captured votes |
| Committee quality | 14 | 15 | All four committees 100% independent; three Audit Financial Experts; minor deduction for Weeks' Corning CEO dual role on Audit |
| RPT and group-leakage protection | 20 | 20 | No material related-party transactions; no promoter group; no leakage channel; disqualifier scan clean; ordinary-course director-affiliate payments judged immaterial |
| Shareholder voting rights | 14 | 15 | Majority voting, annual elections, no dual-class, no supermajority barriers, proxy access, 25% special-meeting threshold — strong rights package; -1 for Board's unilateral board-size authority |
| AGM opposition / minority protection | 5 | 10 | Persistent 22% say-on-pay opposition over two consecutive years (exceeding 20% threshold), ISS three-year consecutive opposition, plateaued improvement; partially offset by evidence of engagement and compensation-practice reform |
| **Total** | **85** | **100** | No disqualifier cap applies; partial-data note: full committee membership from filed proxy not confirmed from pool sources — web-sourced proxy relied upon (confidence 3); RPT section confirmed Green from 10-K |

**Shareholder Friendliness Score: 85 / 100 — "Adequate to Strong"**

The score sits at the boundary between adequate and strong governance. The structural features (no dual-class, no pill, no staggered board, majority voting) are genuinely shareholder-friendly. The drag comes from the persistent 22% say-on-pay opposition that has not been resolved after three years of effort, and the long-tenure concern with Stonesifer. No entrenchment mechanism is in place.

## management-governance / 06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md

_Source: `06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md`_

# Candor & Disclosure Quality — AMZN

**Reporting standard:** US GAAP. **Currency:** USD. **Fiscal year end:** December 31. **Filing regime:** US SEC. **As of:** 2026-07-04.

**Data available:** Five earnings transcripts (Q2 2025–Q1 2026), three annual shareholder letters (FY2023–FY2025), FY2025 10-K, FY2024 10-K, FY2023 10-K, Q1 2026 10-Q, Capital IQ Financials / Estimates exports. Cross-module inputs: `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` (available), `earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` (available), `management-governance/01_management-and-track-record.md` (available). **No proxy (DEF 14A) in pool** — compensation discussion not available; this affects the non-GAAP section only marginally (Amazon does not issue formal adjusted-EBIT tables) and does not cap this agent.

---

## 1. Owning the Misses

From `01_management-and-track-record`, the checkable promises over FY2023–FY2025 were: AWS re-acceleration, International profitability, cost-to-serve reduction, operating income recovery, and the FCF compression that accompanied the AI capex surge.

| Miss (from 01) | How It Was Communicated | Owned or Obscured? | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Q3 2025 operating income below guidance — reported $17.4B vs guidance of $19.0B–$22.0B (high end); shortfall driven by $2.5B FTC settlement and $1.8B severance ($4.3B total charges) | Management led with the number and immediately quantified both special charges: "Operating income was $17.4 billion, but would have been over $21 billion, if not for 2 special Q3 expenses, $2.5 billion for an FTC settlement and $1.8 billion for estimated severance costs" — CFO also stated the two charges, their line item recording, and that ex-charges operating income was $21.7B, "or $1.2 billion above the high end of our guidance range." No hedging language was used before the charges were disclosed. | **Owned plainly.** Both the headline miss and the underlying causes were stated directly and quantified in the first paragraph of prepared remarks. No vague "headwinds" framing. The Q&A invited questions on it without deflection. | Q3 2025 Earnings Call transcript, Oct 30, 2025, pp.4–8 (Jassy prepared remarks; Olsavsky financial update) |
| Q4 2025 operating income below guidance top end — reported $25.0B vs guidance of $23.0B–$28.0B; missed high end — actually in range but weighed down by $2.4B in special charges (Italy tax $1.1B, severance $0.73B, asset impairments $0.61B) | CFO immediately named all three special charges with their dollar amounts, recording line items, and segment impact in the second paragraph of financial remarks. EPS was exactly in-line (0.00% surprise). | **Owned plainly.** Same pattern as Q3: three charges itemized immediately, no burying. | Q4 2025 Earnings Call transcript, Feb 5, 2026, pp.8–9 (Olsavsky financial update) |
| FY2025 strict FCF compressed sharply — $7.7B strict, $11.2B company-disclosed, vs $32.9B and $38.2B in FY2024 | This was explicitly pre-signaled in the FY2024 Shareholder Letter (Apr 2025): "our customers, shareholders, and business will be well-served by investing aggressively now," with the AI capex cycle framed explicitly as a period of intentional FCF compression. On the Q4 2025 call, Olsavsky confirmed full-year company-disclosed FCF of $11.2B without spin. On the Q1 2026 call, when pressed on FCF guardrails and ROIC, Jassy referenced the prior AWS capex cycle as evidence rather than deflecting. | **Owned plainly and proactively.** The compression was both pre-disclosed and then confirmed without downplaying. Management cited trailing 12-month FCF in every transcript — not hiding the number. | FY2024 Annual Report (Shareholder Letter, warning, Apr 2025); FY2025 10-K, MD&A p.28 (outcome); Q4 2025 Earnings Call, Feb 5, 2026, p.8; Q1 2026 Earnings Call, Apr 29, 2026, pp.8–10 |
| Q1 2026 EPS beat very large (+69.5%) — partly inflated by Anthropic investment mark-to-market; no genuine miss here, but the question is whether the beat was framed honestly | CFO on the Q3 2025 call explicitly called out the $9.5B Anthropic pretax gain as "investment activity not related to Amazon's ongoing operations" and included it in non-operating income. The FY2025 10-K, Item 7, p.27 discloses the $15.3B investment gain as a separate line with a footnote explaining the Anthropic reclassification. Management does not trumpet the EPS number in prepared remarks — it focuses on operating income and revenue. | **Candid.** Management consistently directs investors toward operating income and CFO, not reported EPS. Investment gains are disclosed separately in every period they occur. | Q3 2025 Earnings Call transcript, Oct 30, 2025, p.8 (Olsavsky on Anthropic); FY2025 10-K, Item 7, p.27 |
| Severance / restructuring costs recur in every recent year — $62M (FY2021), $163M (FY2022), $767M (FY2023), $763M (FY2024), $4.6B (FY2025) — treated as one-off each time | Management described Q3 2025 severance as "role eliminations" and Q4 2025 severance as a separate charge. Neither transcript explicitly addressed the multi-year pattern. The 10-K Item 7 discloses the amounts but does not acknowledge the pattern explicitly. | **Mixed — not obscured but not fully owned.** Each charge is disclosed and quantified. The recurring nature of the baseline ($700M–$800M even in "normal" years) is not discussed as a structural cost. Calling these "one-off" in every cycle is an implicit overstatement that management has not addressed head-on. See RF-DISC-002 assessment below. | FY2025 10-K, Item 7, p.27; earnings/06_earnings-quality.md §5 |

**Summary:** Management owns genuine one-time misses immediately and quantitatively. The one candor gap is the handling of recurring restructuring charges — each one is disclosed but the pattern is never discussed as quasi-structural.

---

## 2. Non-GAAP / Adjustment Aggressiveness

Amazon does not publish a formal adjusted EBITDA or adjusted EBIT table. Its only official non-GAAP metric is Free Cash Flow (reconciled on FY2025 10-K p.28). Analysis uses `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` as the primary cross-check.

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| **Recurring "one-off" addbacks** | Amazon does not publish adjusted operating income tables, so there is no formal addback list. However, management verbally presents "ex-charges" operating income in transcripts when material charges occur — Q3 2025 ($4.3B charges) and Q4 2025 ($2.4B charges). This verbal adjustment is reasonable and disclosed. The concern is that restructuring/severance has appeared in every recent year at a baseline of ~$700M–$800M (FY2023, FY2024) and spiked in FY2025 ($4.6B with non-recurring components). Management treats each year's charge as a one-off; the cumulative pattern is not acknowledged. | Q3 2025 transcript, Oct 30, 2025, pp.7–8; Q4 2025 transcript, Feb 5, 2026, p.9; earnings/06_earnings-quality.md §5 (five-year restructuring pattern) |
| **GAAP-to-adjusted gap (magnitude / trend)** | The most important gap is at the reported EPS level: FY2025 reported diluted EPS of $7.17 vs Capital IQ Normalized EPS of $4.70 — a 34.5% inflation from the $15.3B pre-tax investment gain (Anthropic reclassification + Rivian). This recurs: FY2021 +$14.7B gain, FY2022 −$15.9B loss, FY2025 +$15.3B gain. At the operating income line (the number management focuses on), the GAAP-to-adjusted gap is minor: FY2025 GAAP operating income was $80.0B; management's verbal "ex-charges" figure for Q3 and Q4 combined amounts to ~$86.7B ($80.0B + $4.3B Q3 + $2.4B Q4), a ~8% uplift. | earnings/06_earnings-quality.md §7; FY2025 10-K, Item 7, p.27; Capital IQ Income Statement |
| **Does adjusted framing flatter reality?** | For operating income: the verbal adjustments are defensible — they strip out genuine one-time legal settlements ($2.5B FTC, $1.1B Italy tax) and asset impairments. Calling all severance non-recurring is aggressive but not fraudulent. For EPS: Amazon does not formally issue adjusted EPS guidance. The $7.17 reported EPS overstates operating earnings quality by 34%; this is a genuine concern, but Amazon's management consistently directs attention to operating income and CFO rather than net income or reported EPS, which partially mitigates the risk that the misleading reported EPS number is being actively promoted. The two consecutive server useful-life changes (5→6yr Jan 2024, 6→5yr Jan 2025) are disclosed fully on 10-K Note 1, p.41–42 and p.47, though the back-and-forth is unusual for a $301B+ asset base. | earnings/06_earnings-quality.md §7, §8 (Accounting Trap Checklist: change in useful life, score 35/100 severity); FY2025 10-K, Note 1, p.41–42 |
| **SBC exclusion from "adjusted" figures** | SBC of $19.5B in FY2025 (2.7% of revenue; 24.3% of EBIT) is excluded from Capital IQ Normalized EPS. Amazon does not formally promote adjusted EPS excluding SBC, but analyst models universally strip it. At $1.80/share, it is a real economic cost worth flagging. Amazon does not attempt to hide SBC: it is prominently disclosed in Note 4 (p.53 of FY2025 10-K) and is a separate CFO reconciliation item. | earnings/06_earnings-quality.md §8; FY2025 10-K, Note 4, p.53 |

**Non-GAAP verdict: Conservative at the operating income level; noisy at the reported EPS level due to inherent equity investment accounting.** Management does not run an aggressive addback program. The single largest distortion (Anthropic mark-to-market) is structural to the business and fully disclosed, though the size of the swing ($15.3B in FY2025, −$15.9B in FY2022) is large enough to materially mislead anyone relying on headline EPS.

---

## 3. Disclosure Transparency

Amazon discloses three reportable segments (North America, International, AWS) with revenue, operating income, and depreciation broken out per Note 10 of the 10-K (p.67–68 of the FY2025 10-K). Within AWS, management provides an annualized revenue run rate and backlog ($364B at Q1 2026 close, stated on Q1 2026 earnings call and confirmed in the 10-Q) but does not break out AI vs core AWS revenue — a notable gap given the scale of AI claims. The company separately discloses advertising services revenue ($56.0B in FY2025), which has become one of its highest-margin businesses, as a separate revenue line but not as a segment with disclosed operating income. Free cash flow is reconciled in the 10-K. KPI disclosure has been consistent period-over-period: the same segment structure, the same revenue breakdowns, and the same FCF reconciliation appear in each annual filing. There is no reduction in transparency over the period reviewed. The one watch point is the deliberate non-disclosure of capex split between maintenance and growth — management acknowledges this limitation in every transcript but does not rectify it, which is a recurring analyst frustration. Overall segment economics are accessible and consistent; the main gap is the absence of an AI-specific revenue or margin disclosure within AWS, which would be the single most useful new disclosure given the $200B capex bet.

---

## 4. Tone in Bad Times

Q3 2025 was the worst recent quarter by operating income miss: $17.4B reported vs guidance of $19.0B–$22.0B high end, driven by a $4.3B combined special charge. The tone was candid. Jassy led his prepared remarks by stating the reported number, then immediately explaining the two charges with dollar amounts — not leading with the "adjusted" figure or the AWS acceleration. The Q&A on the Q3 2025 call addressed the FTC settlement directly (one question from Justin Post implicitly sought confidence in capacity and demand, which Jassy answered substantively). No question on the FTC settlement itself appeared in the Q&A transcript, which likely reflects that management had quantified it so clearly in the prepared remarks that analysts had no follow-up ambiguity. Q4 2025 adopted the same pattern: three charges named and quantified in Olsavsky's second paragraph, before any business update. On the Q4 2025 call, analysts pressed on FCF guardrails and ROIC, and Jassy gave a direct answer — referencing the prior AWS build cycle, acknowledging uncertainty, and declining to set a specific FCF floor — rather than deflecting. The tone in both calls was promotional in presenting the business opportunity (AI, grocery, Leo) but candid in presenting the financial results, including the charges.

---

## 5. Candor Read

Management's communications in bad periods hold up. When results were below guidance (Q3 2025, Q4 2025), the charges were named, sized, and attributed to their cause in the first substantive paragraph of prepared remarks — not buried in footnotes or discovered by analysts in the Q&A. The FCF compression was pre-disclosed and then confirmed without spin. The single biggest candor signal, positive in nature, is the combination of pre-warning on FCF compression (FY2024 letter, Apr 2025) and then delivering exactly the warned outcome without retroactive framing. The one legitimate concern is the treatment of restructuring costs: $700M–$800M annually in "other operating expense" in FY2023–FY2024 that gets called non-recurring each year, and the FY2025 spike to $4.6B that blends genuine one-offs (FTC, Italy) with what has become a recurring baseline of severance and reorganization. The two-direction server useful-life change (5→6yr in 2024, back to 6→5yr in 2025) is disclosed clearly but has never been explained in a way that acknowledges the earnings-management optics. The no-integrity-flag-routed status from `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan` means there is no unresolved adverse signal compounding this read. **Verdict: candid / high-trust.** The evidence for this verdict is specific: charges disclosed immediately and accurately, FCF compression pre-signaled, operating income (not reported EPS) foregrounded, and no history of guidance set-to-miss being obscured. The one area short of ideal is the recurring-restructuring pattern.

| Q&A transparency | 13 | 15 | Analysts' FCF ROIC and guardrail questions answered directly on Q4 2025 and Q1 2026 calls. FTC settlement was fully disclosed before Q&A. | Specificity vs boilerplate | 9 | 10 | Management letters and transcripts include specific metrics (AWS backlog $364B, Rufus MAU +115%, Trainium2 +150% QoQ) alongside investment theses. Deduction: the guidance boilerplate ("inherently unpredictable") is standard, not unique. One minor concern: the capex/FCF recovery thesis relies on the prior AWS cycle as an analogy — cited but not quantitatively comparable. [Q1 2026 transcript pp.4–9; FY2025 Shareholder Letter pp.1–16] |
